Via this diagram, the
G
-invariant primitive
f_{L}
induces a global function on the Borel construction
L_{G}
(by lifting to
L\times 0_{EG}
and descending to
L_{G}
). By construction, the restriction of this function to every fiber of
\pi_{G}
agrees with
f_{L}
itself. Clearly, the same argument applies to each finite-dimensional approximation
L(N)
, and these constructions are compatible with respect to the approximation maps.

Combining this observation with Lemma
3.3
, we conclude that each fiber
L\subset L(N)
is an exact Lagrangian with primitive given by
f_{L}
with respect to the fiberwise restriction of Liouville form
\lambda_{X(N)}
.

3.3.
Classifying spaces as Hilbert manifolds

In the previous subsection, we used a finite-dimensional approximation
EG(N)
to
EG
, where each
EG(N)
is a closed manifold. The purpose of this subsection is to provide an explicit and “workable” model for these approximations and to develop a corresponding Morse framework that meets the requirements for applications in Floer theory. We follow a similar strategy as in
[
33
]
.

Let
G
be a compact connected Lie group,
G
can be embedded into
U(k)
for some sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
U(k)
acts freely on the contractible Stiefel variety
V(k,\infty)
, the restriction of this action to
G
remains free. Thus
V(k,\infty)
serves as a model for the universal
G
-bundle
EG
, which we approximate by

(3.11)

EG(N)\coloneqq V(k,N+k).

The corresponding classifying space and its finite-dimensional approximations are obtained by taking the quotient by
G
:

\displaystyle BG(N)\coloneqq EG(N)/G.

The infinite Stiefel variety
V(k,\infty)
is an infinite dimensional Hilbert manifold and so is
BG
.

Definition 3.5
.

A vector field
\mathcal{V}
is said to be
pseudo-gradient
adapted to a Morse function
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

in a Morse chart around a critical point,
\mathcal{V}
coincides with the standard negative gradient with respect to the Euclidean metric; and

(2)

df(\mathcal{V})\leq 0
where equality holds if and only if at a critical point of
f
.

The key ingredient here is that
BG(N)
admits a rich supply of Morse functions together with adapted pseudo-gradient vector fields that respect the system of finite-dimensional approximations. To carry this out, we begin by analyzing the fibration structure of
BG
.

Consider the natural projection from the Stiefel variety to the complex Grassmannian,

(3.12)

\displaystyle EG=V(k,\infty)\rightarrow\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)

which sends a
k
-frame to the subspace spanned by that frame. This projection descends to a fibration

(3.13)

\displaystyle U(k)/G\hookrightarrow BG\xrightarrow{\pi_{BG}}\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty).

These projections are compatible with the embeddings of
EG(N)
and
BG(N)
respectively. The corresponding projection from
BG(N)
to
\operatorname{Gr}(k,N+k)
is denoted by
\pi_{BG(N)}
.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
be a closed subspace, and denote by
P_{V}
the orthogonal projection onto
V
. Let
\mathcal{L}(E,F)
denote the space of continuous linear maps from a Banach space
E
to
F
, and write
\left\lVert T\right\rVert
for the operator norm of
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,F)
. The assignment
V\rightarrow P_{V}
induces an embedding
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{\infty},\mathbb{C}^{\infty})
, and hence we may define a metric on
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)
by

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(V_{1},V_{2})\coloneqq\left\lVert P_{V_{1}}-P_{V_{1}}\right\rVert.

This makes
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)
a complete metric space. Moreover, one can show that the unit ball is contractible. The infinite complex Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)
is also a Hilbert manifold.

To construct Morse functions on each finite dimensional approximation
BG(N)
, we begin by constructing Morse functions on
\mathrm{Gr}(k,N+k)
that satisfy the desired properties and then lift their gradient vector fields to pseudo-gradient vector fields on
BG(N)
via the fibration (
3.13
) and the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
F\hookrightarrow E\xrightarrow{\pi}B
be a locally trivial fibration, where
F
and
B
are connected compact finite-dimensional manifolds. Fix an auxiliary connection with a Riemannian metric of the form
\pi^{*}g_{B}\oplus g_{F}
where
g_{B}
is a Riemannian metric on
B
, and
g_{F}
is a fiberwise metric. We further assume that these metrics are of Euclidean type in local coordinates near critical points of the Morse functions introduced below
1
1

1

Technically, this assumption is not required for the current lemma if one drops the locally Euclidean assumption from Definition
3.5
; it will be used later when discussing the existence of a CW complex structure, see Remark
4.23
.
.

Let
f_{B}:B\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a Morse function on the base. Choose a function
f_{E}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying the condition: in a local trivialization
U_{i}\times F\cong\pi^{-1}(U_{i})
, one has

(3.15)

\displaystyle f_{E}=\operatorname{pr}_{F}^{*}f_{F}.

where
f_{F}:F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a Morse function and
\operatorname{pr}_{F}:U_{i}\times F\to F
denotes the projection to the fiber.

Then there exists a function
F_{E}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the vector field

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\coloneqq(-\nabla f_{B})^{\#}-\nabla^{v}f_{E}

is a pseudo-gradient vector filed for
F_{E}
. Here
(-\nabla f_{B})^{\#}
is the horizontal lift the negative gradient vector field of
f_{B}
with respect to the connection determined by the metric defined above, and
\nabla^{v}f_{E}
denotes the fiberwise gradient vector field of
f_{E}
.

Proof.

Choose a local trivialization
U_{i}\times F
around each critical point
c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{B})
such that
f_{E}
if of the form (
3.15
). Let
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a bump function supported in
U_{i}
identically equal to
1
near
c_{i}
. Define a function
F_{E}
by

\displaystyle F_{E}\coloneqq\pi^{*}f_{B}+\sum_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{B})}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}f_{F},

where the constants
\epsilon_{i}>0
are taken sufficiently small, and
f_{F}
is viewed as a function defined on
U_{i}\times F
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathrm{supp}(d\phi_{i})
lies in an annulus centered at
c_{i}
. Because
F
is compact, for sufficiently small
\epsilon_{i}
, no new critical points are created. Moreover, in a neighborhood of each
c_{i}
on which
\phi_{i}\equiv 1
, both
\nabla f_{B}
and
\nabla^{v}f_{F}
are gradient vector fields, and the connection induced by the metric agrees with the trivial connection
\ker(\operatorname{pr}_{F})_{*}
on
U_{i}\times F
near the critical points. Thus, the vector field
\mathcal{V}
coincides in this region with the genuine gradient vector field of a quadratic form with respect to the Euclidean metric, provided that
f_{B}
and
f_{F}
themselves enjoy this property.

To verify the Condition (2) of Definition
3.5
, we compute

(3.17)

\displaystyle dF_{E}(\mathcal{V})=-\left\lVert\nabla f_{B}\right\rVert^{2}-\sum_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{B})}\epsilon_{i}d\phi_{i}(\nabla f_{B})f_{F}-\sum_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{B})}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}\left\lVert\nabla^{v}f_{F}\right\rVert^{2}.

Since
d\phi_{i},\nabla f_{B}
and
f_{F}
are bounded on the support of
d\phi_{i}
, for sufficiently small
\epsilon_{i}
, it follows that
dF_{E}(\mathcal{V})\leq 0
, with equality holding only at the critical points of
F_{E}
.
∎

Unfortunately, Lemma
3.6
does not extend to infinite-dimensional Hilbert manifolds. The obstruction is that a closed ball in a Hilbert space is compact if and only if the Hilbert space itself is finite-dimensional. Thus, the second term in (
3.17
) can no longer be uniformly bounded; on the other hand, it may happen that
F_{E}
acquires additional critical points not originating from those of
f_{F}
and
f_{B}
.

Nevertheless, for the purposes of the Borel construction, the following theorem is sufficient. The key point is that all the finite-dimensional approximation are compact, and all Morse-theoretic constructions take place at these finite levels before passage to the colimit.
1
1

1

An alternative strategy would be to verify directly that vector fields of the form (
3.16
) on a Hilbert manifold satisfy the analytic properties required in Morse theory, which is beyond the scope of the present paper.

Theorem-Definition 3.7
.

Let
BG
and its finite-dimensional approximations
BG(N)
be as described above. A family of Morse-Smale pairs
\{(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})\}
consisting of Morse functions
f_{N}
and pseudo-gradient vector fields
\mathcal{V}_{N}
is said to be admissible if following conditions hold:

(1)

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an inclusion of critical points sets:

\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})\subset\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N+1}).

(2)

For any
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
, the stable manifold satisfy
W^{s}(f_{N+1},\mathcal{V}_{N+1};c)\times_{BG(N+1)}BG(N)=W^{s}(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N};c)
.

(3)

For any
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
, the image of the unstable manifold
W^{u}(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N};c)
in
BG(N)
coincides with
W^{u}(f_{N+1},\mathcal{V}_{N+1};c)
in
BG(N+1)
.

(4)

For any
c\in BG(N+1)\setminus BG(N)
, the negative gradient flow line
\gamma_{t}(c)
satisfies
\gamma_{t}(c)\not\in BG(N)
for all
t\geq 0
.

(5)

For each
l\geq 0
, there exists an integer
N(l)>0
such that

|c|>l\quad\text{for all }N\geq N(l)\text{ and }c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})\setminus\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N-1}).

Moreover, for any sequence of strictly increasing negative real numbers
(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)
, one can construct such a family of Morse functions.

Proof.

We begin by recalling the construction of a Morse function on the complex Grassmannian; see
[
50
,
65
,
51
]
for details.

Let
-a_{1}>-a_{2}>\cdots>-a_{n}>\cdots>0
be a strictly increasing sequence of negative real numbers, and write

\displaystyle A\coloneqq\mathrm{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},\cdots),\quad A_{n}\coloneqq\mathrm{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

The function

(3.18)

\displaystyle f_{A_{n}}(V)\coloneqq\mathrm{Re}\left(\operatorname{Tr}(A_{n}P_{V})\right)

is a Morse function on
\operatorname{Gr}(k,n)
(We adopt the cohomological convention, so
f_{A_{n}}
differs from that in
[
50
]
by a negative sign). The critical points of
f_{A_{n}}
are
A_{n}
-invariant
k
-planes, which are nondegenerate iff
A_{n}
is nondegenerate. Moreover, the negative gradient flow of
f_{A_{n}}
though a
k
-plane
V
in
the natural metric is given by

(3.19)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}(V)=e^{-A_{n}t}V=\operatorname{diag}(e^{-a_{1}t},\cdots,e^{-a_{n}t})V.

Thus, we obtain a Morse function
f_{A}
on
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)
whose restriction to each stratum is
f_{A_{k+N}}
. There is an natural inclusion of critical point sets obtained by identifying
A_{k+N}
-invariant
k
-planes with
A_{k+N+1}
-invariant
k
-planes. It is straightforward to verify that
f_{A}
satisfies the conditions (2) (3) and (4) from the explicit description of the gradient flow (
3.19
). For condition (5), note that for any critical point
p
contained in
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)\setminus\operatorname{Gr}(k,k+N)
, we have
|p|>2N
, see
[
50
]
for an explicit computation of the Morse index.

Next, let
f_{U(k)/G}
be a Morse function on the fiber
U(k)/G
. Choose an open cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
of
BG
together with a partition of unity subordinate to it
\{\rho_{\alpha}\}
(the existence of such a partition for Hilbert manifolds is proved in
[
36
, Section 3]
). We then define

\displaystyle F_{U(k)/G}=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}f_{U(k)/G},

where both
\rho_{\alpha}
and
f_{U(k)/G}
are understood to be defined on local trivializations
U(k)/G\times U_{\alpha}
.

Fix a connection on the fibration (
3.13
) and choose a corresponding Riemannian metric of the form
\pi_{BG}^{*}g_{\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)}\oplus g_{U(k)/G}
(cf. Remark
4.23
). We then consider the vector field

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\infty}\coloneqq(-\nabla f_{A})^{\#}+(-\nabla^{v}F_{U(k)/G})

where
(-\nabla f_{A})^{\#}
denotes the horizontal lift of the negative gradient vector field of
f_{A}
, and
-\nabla^{v}F_{U(k)/G}
is the fiberwise negative gradient.

We define
\mathcal{V}_{N}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}_{\infty}|_{BG(N)}
, then it follows from the construction that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{N}=(-\nabla f_{A_{k+N}})^{\#}+(-\nabla^{v}F_{U(k)/G}).

Thus we can construct a Morse function
f_{N}
as in Lemma
3.6
. The family
\{(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})\}
clearly satisfies the desired properties, as the projections of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
to
\operatorname{Gr}(k,N+k)
do. Up to a generic perturbation, they also satisfy the Morse-Smale condition. We will perform the perturbations inductively across the approximations, ensuring at each stage that all of the above properties remain intact.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

It is well known that Morse theory on infinite-dimensional Hilbert manifolds is well-defined under suitable analytical assumptions (see, for example,
[
1
,
2
,
58
]
). A standard way to address the difficulties arising from infinite dimensionality is to require that the Morse function
f
satisfies the
Palais-Smale Condition (C)

[
54
]
, which means every sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subset X
for which
f(x_{n})
is bounded and
\left\lVert\nabla f(x_{n})\right\rVert\rightarrow 0
possesses a convergent subsequence. If, in addition, each critical point
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f)
has finite Morse index, then the unstable manifolds are finite-dimensional and admit compactifications in the usual way. We refer the reader to
[
58
]
, where such Morse functions are said to satisfy the
CF-conditions
.

In our setting, we have constructed a Morse function on
\operatorname{Gr}(k,\infty)
whose critical points all have finite index. Moreover, the negative pseudo-gradient flow of
f_{A}
is explicitly defined for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and is either unbounded or convergent to a limit point—behavior that is stronger than required by the Palais-Smale condition.

Example 3.9
.

Let
G=\mathbb{S}^{1}
, so that
k=1
and
\operatorname{Gr}(1,N+1)=\mathbb{CP}^{N}
. For a point
V=[z]\in\mathbb{CP}^{N}
, the corresponding projection operator
P_{V}
is represented by the normalized outer product

\displaystyle P_{V}=\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}zz^{*}.

The fibers of the projection (
3.13
) are
0
-dimensional. Hence, the Morse function takes the form

f_{N}([z])=\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N+1}a_{i}|z_{i}|^{2}

which is the standard Morse function on
\mathbb{CP}^{N}
. Consider a point of the form

[z]=[z_{1}:\cdots:z_{i}:1:0:\cdots:0]^{T}.

Then, we see that
[z]
lies in the unstable manifold of the critical point of Morse index
2i
. Indeed, we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\gamma_{t}([z])=\operatorname{diag}(e^{(-a_{1}+a_{i+1})t},\cdots,e^{(-a_{i}+a_{i+1})t},1,\cdots)[z]=[0:\cdots:1:0:\cdots 0].

All the Morse indices are even, and the unstable manifold coincide with the Schubert cells.

The situation for a more general unitary group
U(k)
is similar. Let
I\coloneqq\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\}\subset\{1,2,\cdots\}
. The unstable manifold corresponding to the
I
-th critical point consists of matrices of the form

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}*&\cdots&*\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
*&&\vdots\\
1&&\vdots\\
&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}.

One sees that the dimension of the unstable manifold (and hence the Morse index) is given by
(i_{1}-1)+\cdots(i_{k}-k)
. This provides an alternative verification of Condition (5) in Theorem-Definition
3.7
.

4.
Equivariant Floer cohomology

4.1.
Almost complex structure

Since the ambient space is non-compact, it is essential to choose almost complex structures that prevent pseudo-holomorphic curves from escaping to infinity. A well-established approach is to use contact type almost complex structures. We now recall this construction and adapt it to the Borel construction.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\mathbb{R}\times V,\omega)
be the symplectization of a contact manifold
V
. An
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
is said to be
contact type
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

J
is invariant under translations in
\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

J(\partial_{r})=R
where
r
is the coordinate of
\mathbb{R}
, and
R
is the Reeb vector field on
V
.

(3)

J(\mathcal{D})\subset\mathcal{D}
where
\mathcal{D}
is the contact distribution.

Theses conditions are equivalent to
dr=\lambda\circ J
where
r=e^{s}
.

Fix a compatible almost complex structure
J_{T^{*}BG(N)}
on
(T^{*}BG(N),\omega_{T^{*}BG(N)})
, the pullback
\pi^{*}_{N}J_{T^{*}BG(N)}
is a natural almost complex structure on the horizontal subbundle
TX(N)^{\operatorname{hor}}
. We will denote this almost complex structure by
J_{T^{*}BG(N)}
for the sake of simplicity.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
J_{T^{*}BG(N)}
be an almost complex structure on
T^{*}BG(N)
which is of contact type near infinity. We consider almost complex structures on
X(N)
of the form

\displaystyle J_{X(N)}=\begin{pmatrix}J_{T^{*}BG(N)}&0\\
0&J_{F}\end{pmatrix},

viewed as a block matrix with respect to the splitting

TX(N)=TX(N)^{\operatorname{hor}}\oplus TX(N)^{\operatorname{vert}}.

Moreover, we choose a subset
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)\subset X(N)
which is proper over
T^{*}BG(N)
such that on the complement
X(N)\setminus X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, the restriction of
J_{X(N)}
to each fiber is of contact type.

The almost complex structure satisfying the above conditions is called
admissible
.

It follows from the construction that
\pi_{N}
is
(J_{X(N)},J_{T^{*}BG(N)})
-holomorphic and the space of admissible almost complex structures is contractible. Therefore, for the Borel spaces
\{X(N)\}
, we may choose a family of admissible almost complex structures
\{J_{X(N)}\}
such that

\displaystyle J_{X(N)}|_{X(N-1)}=J_{X(N-1)},\quad D\pi_{N}\circ J_{X(N)}=J_{T^{*}BG(N)}\circ D\pi_{N}.

4.2.
Family of Hamiltonians

Recall that we use the coordinate transformation
r=e^{s}
on the cylindrical end. Let
(Y,\alpha)
be a contact manifold, and let
R_{\alpha}
be the corresponding Reeb vector field. We consider its positive half symplectization
(Y\times(1,\infty),d(r\alpha))
. Let
h:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function that is invariant under the Reeb flow. Then the Hamiltonian function
H\coloneqq h(y)r
defines the Hamiltonian vector field

\displaystyle X_{H}(y,r)=h(y)R_{\alpha}(y,r).

We refer to such functions as
linear Hamiltonians
and the function
h(y)
is called the
slope functions
. It is straightforward to verify that
X_{H}
satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

ZH=H
;

(2)

dr(X_{H})=0
;

(3)

H=\lambda(X_{H})

where
Z
and
\lambda
are the Liouville vector fields and Liouville form respectively.

Definition 4.3
.

Fix a family of Morse-Smale pairs
\{(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})\}
as in Theorem
3.7
. Let
\{H(N)\}
be a family of Hamiltonian functions, with each
H(N)\in C^{\infty}(X(N))
.
We say that this family is
admissible
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

The restriction of
H(N+1)
to
X(N)
agrees with
H(N)
, i.e.
H(N+1)|_{X(N)}=H(N)
.

(2)

For each
b\in T^{*}BG(N)
, the restriction
H_{b}\coloneqq H(N)|_{\pi_{N}^{-1}(b)}
is linear near infinity. (We omit
N
in the notation, as this condition is independent of
N
by (1).)

(3)

Near each
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
, the Hamiltonian
H_{b}
takes the form

H_{b}=\operatorname{pr}_{X}^{*}H_{c},

where
\operatorname{pr}_{X}:U\times X\to X
is the projection map in a local trivialization, and
H_{c}
is a linear Hamiltonian defined on
X
.

(4)

For any two critical points
c_{0},c_{1}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
, the parallel transport along the flow line of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
connecting
c_{0}
to
c_{1}
does
not
send a Hamiltonian chord of
H_{c_{0}}
between
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
to a Hamiltonian chord of
H_{c_{1}}
between
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
.

For any
b\in T^{*}BG(N)
, we denote by
F_{b}
the fiber over
b
, which is Liouville isomorphic to
X
by Lemma
3.3
.

The restriction
i_{b}^{*}\omega_{X(N)}
of the symplectic form to the fiber
F_{b}
agrees with the symplectic form
\omega_{X}
on
X
. Hence, the fiberwise Hamiltonian vector field of
H(N)
over a critical point
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
coincides with the usual Hamiltonian vector field on
X
with respect to
\omega_{X}
. Moreover, since the parallel transport preserves the Lagrangian submanifolds, conditions (4) is well-defined.

Remark 4.4
.

In the Definition
4.2
of the family of almost complex structures, we require that the almost complex structure is of contact type outside a region
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)\subset X(N)
that is proper over the base. By enlarging
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
if necessary, we may assume that the Hamiltonian
H(N)
is linear outside the same region.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
L_{0},L_{1}\subset X
be exact cylindrical
G
-invariant Lagrangians. For an admissible family
\{f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N},H(N)\}
associated to their Borel constructions, we say that it is
compatible with
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
if, for each level
N\in\mathbb{N}
and each critical point
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
, the fiberwise time-
1
Hamiltonian flow
\varphi_{H_{c}}^{1}(L_{0})
intersect transversely with
L_{1}
in the fiber
F_{c}
and their intersection set is compact. In this case, the
Borel Floer complex
is defined by

(4.1)

CF(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\coloneqq(c,x)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{K}\cdot\mathbf{x},

where each generator
\mathbf{x}\coloneqq(c,x)
consists of a critical point
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
and a Hamiltonian
1
-chord
x
of
H_{c}
with endpoints on
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
.

As usual, the
action functional
at a generator
\mathbf{x}=(c,x)
is defined by

1
1

1

A pair
(c,x)
itself is not a critical point of
\mathcal{A}(c,x)\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{c}(x)+f_{N}(c)
, as the Hamiltonian term
H_{c}
depends on
c\in BG(N)
:

\mathcal{A}_{c}(x)\coloneqq f_{L_{1}}(x(1))-f_{L_{0}}(x(0))+\int_{0}^{1}-x^{*}\lambda_{X}+H_{c}(x(t))dt.

The definition make sense because
x
is trapped in a fiber
F_{c}\simeq X
.

Lemma 4.6
.

The choice of compatible admissible families in Definition
4.5
forms a Baire set. Moreover one can choose the linear Hamiltonians to have the constant slopes
a\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
(with
a=0
understood as the case of a compactly supported Hamiltonian).

Proof.

For the Morse-Smale pair
\{f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N}\}
part, note that diagonal matrices with distinct eigenvalues form a dense subset in the spectrum of diagonal matrices (cf. Theorem
3.7
), and that increasing sequences of real numbers constitute a convex set.

To construct the family of Hamiltonians, take a local trivialization around each critical point
c_{i}
and define
H_{b}
near
c_{i}
as in (3) of Definition
4.3
, extending it to all of
X(N)
. For a generic choice of
H
, the image
\phi^{1}_{H_{c}}(L_{0})
intersects
L_{1}
transversely and is contained in a compact set. Since for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the critical set
\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
is finite, we can inductively perturb Hamiltonians in compact subsets so that (4) of Definition
4.3
is satisfied.

The intersection of all such choices over
N
is a Baire set by the Baire category theorem.
∎

4.3.
Gradings and signs

Under the assumption that the bicanonical bundle
\kappa^{2}
of
X
admits a trivialization (for example, when
2c_{1}(X)=0
), we work with cohomologically unital,
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
A_{\infty}
-categories over an algebraically closed field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
2
. See
[
63
]
for a detailed construction.

In general, for the family version of Floer theory, one only obtains a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading, and there is no canonical refinement to a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading. This is because we make no prior assumptions on the automorphism group of
X
. However, in the context of Borel construction, there is a canonical way to obtain such a refinement.

To define the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading, we fix a fiber of the projection
X(N)\rightarrow T^{*}BG(N)
, and choose a trivialization of the bicanonical bundle on the fiber
X
. Since the structure group
G
is connected, it acts trivially on the homotopy class of such a trivialization. Thus, the grading is well-defined on each fiber.

For each generator
\mathbf{x}\coloneqq(c,x)\in CF(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))
, by the above discussion, there are well-define
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings of
c
and
x
. The grading of
|x|
is defined as

\displaystyle|\mathbf{x}|\coloneqq|c|+|x|.

4.4.
Floer theory in Borel construction

Let
L_{0},L_{1}\subset X
be exact cylindrical Lagrangians, and fix a compatible admissible family
\{f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N},H(N)\}
as in Definition
4.5
. Let

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i}=(c_{i},x_{i})\in CF(L_{0},L_{1};X(N)),\quad i=0,1

be two generators of the Borel Floer complex. We abbreviate
H_{(\tau)}\coloneqq H(N)_{\gamma(\tau)}
, where
\gamma
denotes a trajectory of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
; other auxiliary data (such as almost complex structures) are abbreviated in the same way. We impose the boundary condition
\partial_{\tau}H_{(\tau)}\leq 0
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, where the derivative is taken with respect to parallel transport along
\gamma
. Let
\pi_{N}:X(N)\rightarrow T^{*}BG(N)
denote the projection map as before.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
denote the space of pairs

\{(\gamma,u)\mid\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow 0_{BG(N)},u:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\rightarrow X(N)\}

satisfying the following conditions:

(4.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\gamma-\mathcal{V}_{N}=0,\quad\pi_{N}\circ u=\gamma,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\left(du^{\operatorname{vert}}-X_{H_{(\tau)}}\otimes dt\right)^{0,1}_{J_{(\tau,t)}^{\operatorname{vert}}}=0,

(4.4)

\displaystyle u(\tau,0)\in L_{0}(N),\quad u(\tau,1)\in L_{1}(N),

(4.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\gamma(\tau)=c_{0},\quad\lim_{\tau\rightarrow+\infty}\gamma(\tau)=c_{1},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}u(\tau,\cdot)=x_{0},\quad\lim_{\tau\rightarrow+\infty}u(\tau,\cdot)=x_{1}.

Here
du^{\operatorname{vert}}
denotes vertical component of the differential
du
with respect to the connection determined by
\omega_{X(N)}
, and
X_{H_{(\tau)}}
is the fiberwise Hamiltonian vector field. We define

(4.7)

\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))/\mathbb{R}.

From the horizontal equation (
4.2
), the projection
\pi_{N}\circ u
is a Morse trajectory on the zero section of
T^{*}BG(N)
. Therefore, each element of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
is of one of the following two types:

(1)

A Hamiltonian-perturbed pseudo-holomorphic curve bounded by
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
, entirely contained in the fiber
F_{c_{i}}
over the critical points
c_{0},c_{1}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
;

(2)

A continuation-type map associated to a family of Hamiltonians
H_{(\tau)}
, whose projection
\pi_{N}\circ u
is a non-constant Morse trajectory.

The first case corresponds to the situation where
\pi_{N}\circ u
is constant, while the second arises when
\pi_{N}\circ u
is non-constant. Along a given trajectory
\gamma
, we may trivialize the bundle
X(N)
and view the vertical component of
u
as a map into a fixed fiber
X
. Under this identification, the boundary condition (
4.4
) simply becomes

u(\tau,i)\in L_{i}

for
i\in\{0,1\}
, since the parallel transport preserves the Lagrangian.

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
is the common zero locus of the Fredholm sections defined by the Floer equations (
4.2
)
and (
4.3
). Hence, its expected (virtual) dimension is given by

(4.8)

\mathrm{vdim}\hskip 2.0pt\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))=|\mathbf{x}_{0}|-|\mathbf{x}_{1}|-1.

Theorem 4.8
.

For a generic choice of admissible almost complex structure, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
is a smooth manifold of the expected dimension given in (
4.8
).

Proof.

It is standard that the transversality for moduli space of Floer strips can be achieved by choosing generic time-dependent almost complex structures; see, for example,
[
16
]
and
[
63
, (9k)]
. We briefly recall the argument and explain how it applies in our setting.

The key observation is that for a generic choice of time-dependent almost complex, the associated linearized Cauchy-Riemman operator is surjective, except possibly at constant components, which must be treated separately.

In the present case, the zero set defining the moduli space is the joint zero locus of the two Floer (Morse) equations (
4.2
) and (
4.3
). The corresponding linearized operator decomposes as the direct sum of the linearizations of horizontal and vertical components.

Surjectivity of this operator follows from the surjectivity of each component: the Floer part can be made transverse by a generic perturbation of the almost complex structure, while the Morse part is transverse by the Morse-Smale condition, which holds by construction.

To complete the proof, we must rule out the constant solutions. This is ensured by Condition (4) in Definition
4.3
.
∎

Compactness

We now show that the usual maximum principle is satisfied, ensuring the Gromov-Floer compactification applies.

Theorem 4.9
.

If
\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))\leq 1
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
admits a Gromov compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
.

The proof is almost identical to the classical case. For completeness and reader’s convenience, we include the proof here. A more global formulation of this argument will be presented later in Definition
5.1
.

Since each fiber is non-compact, we must impose additional conditions on the Lagrangian boundary (equivalently, on the Hamiltonian functions) to prevent the strip form escaping to infinity. Recall that we require
\partial_{\tau}H_{(\tau)}\leq 0
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
.

Our approach follows the standard argument using
non-negative moving boundary conditions
. We adapt the following lemma from
[
4
, Lemma 7.2]
(see also
[
19
, Lemma 2.46]
) to our setting.

To define the energy, for later use, we give a more general statement. Let
\Sigma
be a compact Riemman surface with boundary, and
\alpha
a
1
-form satisfying
d\alpha\leq 0
(see (
5.2
) for detailed definitions). We write
\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}
the vertical component of the Liouville form
\lambda_{X(N)}
(for simplicity, we drop the subscripts as it is independent of the stratum). We define the
geometric energy
by

\displaystyle E(u)^{\operatorname{vert}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\|du^{\operatorname{vert}}-X_{H_{(\tau)}}\otimes\alpha\|^{2}=\int_{\Sigma}u^{*}d\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}-u^{*}(dH_{(\tau)})\wedge\alpha

where
dH_{(\tau)}
is taken fiberwise. Integrating by parts, we have

(4.9)

\displaystyle E(u)^{\operatorname{vert}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}u^{*}d\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}-d(u^{*}H_{(\tau)}\wedge\alpha)+\partial_{\tau}(u^{*}H_{(\tau)})d\tau\wedge\alpha+u^{*}H_{(\tau)}d\alpha

(4.10)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Sigma}u^{*}d\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}-d(u^{*}H_{(\tau)}\alpha)

For the inequality, we use the condition
\partial_{\tau}H_{(\tau)}\leq 0
and the subclosedness of
\alpha
. When
\Sigma
is a strip as in Definition
4.7
, applying Stokes’ theorem we obtain

(4.11)

\displaystyle E(u)^{\operatorname{vert}}\leq\mathcal{A}_{c_{0}}(x_{0})-\mathcal{A}_{c_{1}}(x_{1})

which is the standard inequality bounding geometric energy by topological energy.

Remark 4.10
.

When we trivialize the bundle
X(N)
over a given trajectory, the vertical geometric energy coincides with the standard energy of a Floer trajectory with (possibly) moving Lagrangian boundary conditions. The same statement also applies to the action functional.

For a Liouville manifold, the following lemma is sufficient to ensure the compactness of the moduli spaces. In the case of a Liouville sector, the wrapping Hamiltonian we construct is chosen so that
\partial_{\tau}H_{(\tau)}=0
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
; see Lemma-Definition
5.1
. In other words, we do not consider moving boundary condition which are not compactly supported on a Liouville sector.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
u
be a pseudo-holomorphic curve as in Definition
4.7
. Suppose that
u(\partial\Sigma)\subset X(N)\setminus X^{\mathrm{in}}(N),
then either
u
is a locally constant map, or its image lies entirely in
\partial X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
.

Proof.

It follows from Equation (
4.9
) that

(4.12)

\displaystyle E(u)^{\operatorname{vert}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Sigma}u^{*}d\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}-d(u^{*}H_{(\tau)}\alpha)

(4.13)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}u^{*}\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}-u^{*}H_{(\tau)}\alpha

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}u^{*}\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}-u^{*}\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}(X_{H_{(\tau)}})\alpha

(4.15)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}\left(du^{\operatorname{vert}}-X_{H_{(\tau)}}\otimes\alpha\right)

(4.16)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}-\lambda^{\operatorname{vert}}J\left(du^{\operatorname{vert}}-X_{H_{(\tau)}}\otimes\alpha\right)j

(4.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}-dr\left(du^{\operatorname{vert}}-X_{H_{(\tau)}}\otimes\alpha\right)j

(4.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}-drdu^{\operatorname{vert}}j

(4.19)

\displaystyle\leq 0.

The identity in Equation (
4.15
) follows directly from the assumption that
H_{(\tau)}
is linear outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
.
We note that the radius function
r
appearing in Equation (
4.18
) is globally defined outside of
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
since it is
G
-invariant.

This suffices to conclude the argument.
∎

Differential and Equivariant Floer cohomology

We define the Floer differential on
CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))
by

\displaystyle\partial_{N}\mathbf{x}_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{|\mathbf{x}_{0}|=|\mathbf{x}_{1}|+1}\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))\mathbf{x}_{0}.

By the compactness of the moduli space, this differential is well-defined and satisfies
\partial_{N}^{2}=0
. We denote the corresponding Floer cohomology by

\displaystyle HF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))\coloneqq H^{\bullet}(CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N)),\partial_{N}).

Since
\operatorname{Crit}{f_{N}}\subset\operatorname{Crit}{f_{N+1}}
and
H(N+1)|_{X(N)}=H(N)
, there is a natural inclusion of chain complexes

\displaystyle CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))\hookrightarrow CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N+1)).

We define
P_{N+1}
to be the projection map

\displaystyle P_{N+1}:CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N+1))\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N)).

Lemma 4.12
.

The following diagram commutes:

(4.20)

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.95552pt}{-13.98802pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

In other words, the projection map
P_{N}
defines a chain map.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{x}_{i}=(c_{i},x_{i})\in CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))
for
i=0,1
. Each element
(\gamma,u)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
projects to a Morse trajectory
\gamma
on
BG(N)
. The moduli space admits a fibration

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(c_{0},c_{1};\mathcal{V}_{N}),

where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(c_{0},c_{1};\mathcal{V}_{N})
denotes the moduli space of Morse trajectories connecting
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
.

By the properties of the pseudo-gradient vector field
\mathcal{V}_{N}
established in Theorem
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle W^{u}(f_{N+1},\mathcal{V}_{N+1};c_{0})\cap W^{s}(f_{N+1},\mathcal{V}_{N+1};c_{1})=W^{u}(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N};c_{0})\cap W^{s}(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N};c_{1}),

which implies that the count of trajectories is preserved under the inclusion
X(N)\subset X(N+1)
. In particular, for
|x_{0}|=|x_{1}|+1
,

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N+1))=\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N)).

Hence, the Floer differential commutes with the projection
P_{N}
:

\displaystyle P_{N+1}\circ\partial_{N+1}\mathbf{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{x}_{0}|=|\mathbf{x}_{1}|+1\\
c_{0}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N+1})\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N+1))P_{N+1}(\mathbf{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{x}_{0}|=|\mathbf{x}_{1}|+1\\
c_{0}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},P_{N+1}(\mathbf{x}_{1});X(N))\mathbf{x}_{0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{N}\circ P_{N+1}\mathbf{x}_{1}.

Thus, the diagram (
4.20
) commutes and
P_{N}
defines a chain map.
∎

Definition 4.13
.

The
equivariant Lagrangian Floer cohomology
is defined as the inverse limit

\displaystyle HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L_{0},L_{1})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{N}HF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N)).

Recall that an inverse system
\{A_{i},f_{ij}\}_{i\in I}
satisfies the
Mittag-Leffler condition
if for every index
i
, there exists
j\geq i
such that for all
k\geq j
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(f_{ik})=\mathrm{Im}(f_{ij}).

If this condition holds, then the the first derived functor
\varprojlim^{1}
vanishes, and the inverse limit respects the cohomology.

In our case, the inverse system is formed by projections maps which are always surjective. Hence the system satisfies Mittag-Leffler condition. Accordingly, we define the
equivariant Floer cochain complex
by

\displaystyle CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{N}{CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))}.

This complex depends on several choices of auxiliary data. However, up to quasi-isomorphism, it is well-defined and its cohomology agrees with the equivariant Floer cohomology
HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L_{0},L_{1})
.

4.5.
Continuation maps

We now discuss the the natural maps between Floer complexes arising from varying the different choices of auxiliary data such as Hamiltonian functions and almost complex structures. This involves two aspects: the invariance of the Floer cohomology and the wrapped Floer cohomology.

Let
L_{0},L_{1}\subset X
be exact cylindrical Lagrangians. We first fix an admissible family of Morse–Smale paris
\{f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N}\}
. Let
\{H^{-}(N)\}
and
\{H^{+}(N)\}
be admissible families of compatible Hamiltonian functions satisfying

\partial_{\tau}H^{\pm}_{(\tau)}\leq 0.

For a one-parameter family of admissible cylindrical Hamiltonians
\{H_{\tau}(N)\}
, we say it is
monotone homotopy
if satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

H_{\tau}=H_{-}
for
\tau\ll 0
,

(2)

H_{\tau}=H_{+}
for
\tau\gg 0
, and

(3)

\partial_{\tau}((H_{\tau})_{(\tau)})\leq 0
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, where
(H_{\tau})_{(\tau)}\coloneqq H_{\tau}|_{\gamma(\tau)}
and the derivative is taken with respect to parallel transport along a trajectory
\gamma
of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
,

and we define a
continuation map
:

\displaystyle\kappa^{N}_{H^{-},H^{+}}:CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N),H^{+}(N))\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N),H^{-}(N)).

Remark 4.14
.

Let
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},[0,1])
be a monotone increasing function such that
\psi(\tau)=0
for
\tau\ll 0
and
\psi(\tau)=1
for
\tau\gg 0
. This gives rise to a homotopy of Hamiltonians

(4.22)

\displaystyle H_{\tau}=(1-\psi(\tau))H^{-}+\psi(\tau)H^{+},\quad\tau\in\mathbb{R}

which induces an exact Lagrangian isotopy. Moreover, we observe that
H_{\tau}
satisfies
\partial_{\tau}((H_{\tau})_{(\tau)})\leq 0
away from
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
whenever
H^{-}\geq H^{+}
near infinity. In particular, this includes the case where
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
are disjoint near infinity and
H^{\pm}
are compactly supported.

For generators
\mathbf{x}_{-}=(c_{-},x_{-})
and
\mathbf{x}_{+}=(c_{+},x_{+})
of
CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{-}(N),X(N))
and
CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};H^{+}(N),X(N))
respectively, choose a family of admissible almost complex structures, we define the moduli space as follows.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(H_{\tau})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})
denote the space of pairs

\{(\gamma,u)\mid\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow 0_{BG(N)},u:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\rightarrow X(N)\}

satisfying the following conditions:

(4.23)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\gamma-\mathcal{V}_{N}=0,\quad\pi_{N}\circ u=\gamma,

(4.24)

\displaystyle\left(du^{\operatorname{vert}}-X_{(H_{\tau})_{(\tau)}}\otimes dt\right)^{0,1}_{J_{(\tau,t)}^{\operatorname{vert}}}=0,

(4.25)

\displaystyle u(\tau,0)\in L_{0}(N),\quad u(\tau,1)\in L_{1}(N),

(4.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(\gamma(\tau),u(\tau,\cdot))=\mathbf{x}_{-},\quad\lim_{\tau\rightarrow+\infty}(\gamma(\tau),u(\tau,\cdot))=\mathbf{x}_{+}.

Here
du^{\operatorname{vert}}
denotes vertical component of the differential
du
.

The virtual dimension of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(H_{\tau})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})
is given by
|\mathbf{x}_{-}|-|\mathbf{x}_{+}|
. By arguments analogous to Theorem
4.8
and Theorem
4.9
, we obtain the following conclusions:

(1)

For a generic choice of admissible almost complex structure, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(H_{\tau})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})
is a smooth manifold of the expected dimension.

(2)

If
\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(H_{\tau})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})\leq 1
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1};X(N))
admits a Gromov-Floer compactification.

We therefore define
continuation map

(4.27)

\displaystyle\kappa_{H^{-},H^{+}}^{N}(\mathbf{x}_{+})\coloneqq\sum_{|\mathbf{x}_{-}|=|\mathbf{x}_{+}|}\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(H_{\tau})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})\mathbf{x}_{-}.

A standard homotopy argument leads to the following argument.

Proposition 4.16
.

(1)

The linear map
\kappa_{H^{-},H^{+}}^{N}
is a chain map.

(2)

Up to homotopy,
\kappa_{H^{-},H^{+}}^{N}
depends only on the endpoints
H^{-}
and
H^{+}
.

(3)

The induced maps on Floer cohomology satisfy the composition law
F_{H^{-},H^{\prime}}^{N}\circ F_{H^{\prime},H^{+}}^{N}=F_{H^{-},H^{+}}^{N}
, provided the corresponding homotopies are monotone.

Proof.

The first statement follow from analyzing the boundary strata of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(H_{\tau})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})
.

For (2), note that the space of admissible auxiliary data is convex. The standard construction of chain homotopies in Floer theory therefore applies verbatim; see
[
17
,
48
]
for detailed proofs.

Finally, the statement (3) follows from the usual gluing argument.
∎

We now turn our attention to the Borel construction. An argument entirely analogous to that of Lemma
4.12
shows that the continuation maps are compatible with the projection maps in the inverse system, in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

(4.28)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.65556pt}{-13.69919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

This commutativity follows from the fact that the moduli space of continuation trajectories projects to the moduli space of Morse trajectories, and the rest of the argument proceeds exactly as in Lemma
4.12
.

As a consequence, we obtain a continuation map on the level of equivariant Floer cohomology for different choices of Hamiltonian data

\displaystyle F_{H^{-},H^{+}}^{G}:HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};\{H^{+}(N)\})\rightarrow HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};\{H^{-}(N)\}),

which we refer to as the
G-equivariant continuation map
.

Invariance of equivariant Floer cohomology

Suppose that the admissible families of Hamiltonians
\left\{H^{-}(N)\right\}
and
\left\{H^{+}(N)\right\}
agree outside the region
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
for each
N
. In this situation, the monotone homotopy may be defined in either direction, and Proposition
4.16
immediately implies that the resulting equivariant Floer cohomologies coincide.

More generally, if the Hamiltonian isotopy is sufficiently small, for instance when it is supported inside a Weinstein neighborhood of
L
, one can also construct an inverse homotopy of such an isotopy; see for example
[
19
, Lemma 3.21]
and
[
3
, Proposition 5.6]
.

We formalize this as follows.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
\left\{H^{-}(N)\right\}
and
\left\{H^{+}(N)\right\}
be admissible families of Hamiltonians compatible with
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
, and suppose that
H_{-}(N)
agrees with
H_{+}(N)
outside a family of closed subspace
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
. Then the
G
-equivariant continuation map

\displaystyle F_{H^{-},H^{+}}^{G}:HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};\{H^{+}(N)\})\rightarrow HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};\{H^{-}(N)\}),

is an isomorphism.

Definition 4.18
.

For a single exact cylindrical Lagrangian
L\subset X
, we define
HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L,L)
to be the equivariant Floer cohomology computed using any admissible family of Hamiltonians with sufficiently
C^{2}
-small slope.

We now verify that equivariant Floer cohomology is independent of all other admissible auxiliary choices. By the preceding argument, it suffices to fix the slopes of Hamiltonians and vary the remaining data within admissible class.

Let
\{(f^{\pm}_{N},\mathcal{V}^{\pm}_{N})\}
be admissible families of Morse-Smale pairs, where each
f_{N}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\pm}_{N}
are constructed based on strictly increasing sequence of negative real numbers

A^{\pm}\coloneqq\operatorname{diag}\{a^{\pm}_{1},a_{2}^{\pm},\cdots\},

as shown in Theorem
3.7
.

Since the space of such sequences is convex, we may choose a one-parameter family
A_{\tau}
of strictly increasing negative sequences such that
A_{\tau}=A^{-}
for
\tau\ll 0
and
A_{\tau}=A^{+}
for
\tau\gg 0
. As in Theorem
3.7
, this determines a corresponding family of Morse-Smale pairs
\{f_{\tau,N},\mathcal{V}_{\tau,N}\}
on each
BG(N)
, interpolating between
\{(f^{-}_{N},\mathcal{V}^{-}_{N})\}
and
\{(f^{+}_{N},\mathcal{V}^{+}_{N})\}
at the ends.

The chain map between the associated Morse complexes is defined by counting solutions
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow BG(N)
to the gradient equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\gamma=\mathcal{V}_{\tau,N}.

Each trajectory satisfies the same compatibility condition as in Theorem
3.7
, since it is obtained by lifting of gradient vector fields of functions

(4.29)

\displaystyle f_{A_{\tau,N+k}}(V)\coloneqq\mathrm{Re}\left(\operatorname{Tr}(A_{\tau,N+k}P_{V})\right)

defined on the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(k,N+k)
.

Therefore, we can define the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{-},\mathbf{x}_{+};X(N),(\mathcal{V}_{\tau,N})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}})
similarly as in Definition
4.15
. A similar argument to that in Lemma
4.12
shows that the corresponding chain map commutes with the projection map
P_{N}
, and hence induces a well-defined continuation map on equivariant Floer cohomology:

\displaystyle HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};\{\mathcal{V}^{+}_{N}\})\rightarrow HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};\{\mathcal{V}^{-}_{N}\}).

The inverse continuation map is obtained by reversing the family
(\mathcal{V}_{\tau,N})_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
, implying the above map is an isomorphism.

The invariance with respect to other auxiliary data, such as choice of almost complex structures and metrics can be proved in the same way.

Wrapped Floer cohomology as direct limit

With these preparations in place, we are now ready to define the equivariant version of wrapped Floer cohomology. Let
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
be exact cylindrical Lagrangian submanifolds. We consider admissible families of liner Hamiltonians whose slopes are positive constants.

Fix an increasing sequence of positive real numbers
\{s_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}s_{n}=\infty,

and such that, for each
n
,
s_{n}
is not equal to the length of any Reeb chord from
\partial_{\infty}L_{0}
and
\partial_{\infty}L_{1}
over any critical point. For a residual set of almost complex structure, we may therefore define

\displaystyle HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};H_{s_{n}})

as in Section
4.4
.
Whenever
s_{n}\geq s_{m}
, the continuation map constructed in the previous section induces a morphism

\displaystyle HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};H_{s_{m}})\rightarrow HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};H_{s_{n}}).

which makes the collection
\{HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};H_{s_{n}})\}
a direct system.

Definition 4.19
.

The
G
-equivariant wrapped Floer cohomology
is defined as

\displaystyle HW_{G}(L_{0},L_{1})\coloneqq\varinjlim_{s_{n}}HF_{G}(L_{0},L_{1};H_{s_{n}}).

4.6.
G
-equivariant PSS isomorphism

There is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSS}:H^{\bullet}(L)\rightarrow HF^{\bullet}(L,L).

which is called Piunikhin-Salamon-Schwarz (PSS) map.
The modules
HF^{\bullet}(L,K)
and
HF^{\bullet}(K,L)
over it are unital. The aim of this subsection is to enhance the PSS isomorphism to the following identification.

Theorem 4.20

(
G
-equivariant PSS isomorphism)
.

For an embedded
G
-invariant exact cylindrical Lagrangian, there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle H_{G}^{\bullet}(L;\mathbb{K})\cong HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L,L)

which we call the
G
-equivariant PSS isomorphism.

Proof.

The proof is given at the end of this section.

∎

In the case where the
G
-action is free, the equivariant and ordinary cohomologies coincide via the standard isomorphism
H_{G}^{\bullet}(L)\cong H^{\bullet}(L/G)
.
Applying this identification to the equivariant Floer cohomology yields the following corollary, which is a special case of a more general statement in the next section.

Corollary 4.21
.

Let
L\subset\mu^{-1}_{X}(0)
be an embedded,
G
-invariant, exact, cylindrical Lagrangian submanifold.
If the
G
-action on
L
is free, then there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L,L)\;\cong\;HF^{\bullet}(L/G,\,L/G).

Recall that a
local system
on a topological space
X
is a functor from the fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(X)
to the category of abelian groups (in our case, one may always assume the target is the category
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces). More explicitly, it assigns to each point of
X
an abelian group
A_{x}
, and to every homotopy class
[\gamma]
of paths a group homomorphism
\Phi_{[\gamma]}
between the corresponding abelian groups, in a way compatible with the concatenation. We will refer
\Phi_{[\gamma]}
as the
parallel transport
along
\gamma
.

If all the
A_{x}
is a fixed abelian group
A
, and
\Phi_{[\gamma]}
are all identity maps, we say this is a
constant local system with value
A
.

Just as in the usual construction of the PSS isomorphism, we will employ a Morse model as an intermediate step to build the correspondence between Floer cohomology and singular cohomology. The exposition here follows closely
[
30
,
52
,
61
]
.

The
E_{2}
-page of the Leray–Serre spectral sequence

Following
[
47
,
52
]
, we briefly recall the construction of the second page of Leray–Serre spectral sequence. Let
F\hookrightarrow E\xrightarrow{\pi}B
be a locally trivial fibration such that
B
is a CW-complex and both
B
and
F
are path-connected. The second page
LS_{2}^{p,q}
of the Leray–Serre spectral sequence admits an explicit description as

H^{p}(B;\mathcal{H}^{q}(F)),

where
\mathcal{H}^{q}(F)
denotes the local system on
B
whose fiber at a point
b\in B
is
H^{q}(F)
.

For a path
\gamma
contained in a contractible open space, we define the morphism as
\Phi_{[\gamma]}\coloneqq i_{\gamma(1)}^{*}(i_{\gamma(0)}^{*})^{-1}
where

\displaystyle F_{\gamma(0)}\xhookrightarrow{i(0)}\pi^{-1}(U)\xhookleftarrow{i(1)}F_{\gamma(1)}

are the inclusions. For a general path
\gamma
, choose a subdivision
0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{n+1}=1
such that each
\gamma|_{[t_{k},t_{k+1}]}
is contained in a contractible open set. For
x,y\in[0,1]
, let
\Phi_{x,y}\coloneqq\Phi_{\gamma|_{[x,y]}}
. Define
\Phi_{[\gamma]}\coloneqq\Phi_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\circ\cdots\Phi_{t_{0},t_{1}}
. For
|x-z|
sufficiently small, we have
\Phi_{x,y}\circ\Phi_{y,z}=\Phi_{x,z}
. It follows that
\Phi_{[\gamma]}
is independent of the choice of sufficiently fine partitions. The proof of the composition is similar.

A Morse model

In the proof of Theorem
3.7
, we have constructed a vector field
\mathcal{V}_{\infty}
on
BG
by lifting the gradient on the base and adding a vertical gradient component, and we realize this vector field as the pseudo-gradient stratum-wise.

Here we present an alternative viewpoint, following
[
30
]
, by interpreting the construction in terms of a
family of Morse functions
(not to be confused with the family of Morse-Smale pairs used earlier).

Our goal is to build a Morse-theoretic model that realizes the Leray–Serre spectral sequence associated to the fibration

L\hookrightarrow L(N)\xrightarrow{\pi_{N}}BG(N).

Choose a Morse-Smale pair
(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})
on
0_{BG(N)}\subset T^{*}BG(N)
as in Theorem
3.7
. Next, we equip it with a smooth family of functions on the fibers of
f_{N}
, that is, a family of functions

(4.30)

\displaystyle f_{b}:L\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad b\in 0_{BG(N)},

parametrized by points
b\in 0_{BG(N)}
. Moreover, we assume that
f_{b}
is locally constant as a family in a neighborhood of each
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
.

In addition, we choose a corresponding family of Riemannian metrics so that, for each critical point
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
, the corresponding fiberwise function
f_{c}
is Morse. Recall that we have already chosen the metric on
BG(N)
generically so that each pair
(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})
is Morse-Smale, as in Theorem
3.7
.

Since each fiber is noncompact, we further require that
f_{c}
is nongegative, linear near infinity, and has no critical point on the cylindrical end. Morse functions on
L
satisfying these conditions automatically fulfill the Palais-Smale Condition (C) (See Remark
3.8
), ensuring that Morse theory is well-defined in the noncompact setting; because each
f_{c}
is bounded below, its negative gradient flow is defined for all positive time and converges to a critical point; see
[
43
]
.

We denote the family of Morse functions by
\widehat{f}_{N}
and the family of Riemannian metrics by
\widehat{g}_{N}
, which are required to be compatible with the system of approximations in an obvious way.

As before, we shall choose
(\widehat{f}_{N},\widehat{g}_{N})
generically so that the parallel transport does not map a critical point to another critical point, ensuring the generic transversality conditions. For brevity, we will always omit the Riemannian metric from the notation.

The fiberwise Morse cohomology is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded over an algebraically closed field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
2
.

Now, define a bigraded cochain complex
CM^{\bullet,\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})
as follows. The generators are given by

(4.31)

CM^{p,q}\coloneqq\{(c,\alpha)\mid c\in\operatorname{Crit}^{p}(f_{N}),\alpha\in\operatorname{Crit}^{q}(f_{c})\}.

For generators
\mathbf{a}\coloneqq(c_{i},\alpha)
and
\mathbf{b}\coloneqq(c_{j},\beta)
, we define the unparametrized moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N})
to consist of pairs
(\gamma,\varphi)
of the form:

(4.32)

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N})\coloneqq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow 0_{BG(N)}\\
&\varphi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow L(N)\end{aligned}\hskip 2.0pt\middle|\hskip 2.0pt\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\tau}\gamma-\mathcal{V}_{N}=0\\
&\frac{d\varphi^{\mathrm{vert}}}{d\tau}+\nabla^{v}f_{{\gamma(\tau)}}(\varphi(\tau))=0\\
&\pi_{N}\circ\varphi(\tau)=\gamma(\tau)\\
&\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(\gamma,\varphi)(\tau)=(c_{i},\alpha)\\
&\lim_{\tau\rightarrow+\infty}(\gamma,\varphi)(\tau)=(c_{j},\beta)\end{aligned}\right\}/\mathbb{R}.

Here
d\varphi^{\mathrm{vert}}
denotes the vertical component of
d\varphi
. The gradient vector field
\nabla^{v}f_{\gamma(\tau)}(\varphi(\tau))
is taken fiberwise with respect to the chosen fiberwise Riemannian metric.

By trivializing the fibration along
\gamma
, we see that this definition recovers precisely the continuation maps associated with the family of functions
f_{\gamma(\tau)}
, where
f_{\gamma(\tau)}=f_{c_{i}}
for
\tau\ll 0
and
f_{\gamma(\tau)}=f_{c_{j}}
for
\tau\gg 0
. The expected dimension of the moduli space is

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N})

\displaystyle=|\mathbf{a}|-|\mathbf{b}|-1

For each
k\geq 0
, we define the component of the differential
\delta_{k}:CM^{p,q}\rightarrow CM^{p+k,q-k+1}
by setting

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|c_{i}|=p+k,\\
|\alpha|=q-k+1\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N})\mathbf{a}

The total differential is
\delta=\sum_{k\geq 0}\delta_{k}
. A standard compactness and gluing argument shows that
\delta^{2}=0
. We write the (family) Morse cohomology of the total complex
CM^{\bullet}
by

\displaystyle HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})\coloneqq H^{\bullet}\left(CM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N}),\delta\right).

Remark 4.22
.

Recall that a
morphism of spectral sequences

K
is a collection of maps

K_{r}:E_{r}^{\bullet,\bullet}\rightarrow{}^{\prime}E_{r}^{\bullet,\bullet}
for each
r\geq 0
, such that
K_{r}
commutes with the differentials.
If
K_{r}
is an isomorphism on the
E_{r}
-page for some
r
, then it induces isomorphisms on all subsequent pages
E_{s}
for
s\geq r
by five lemma. In particular, if the spectral sequences converge, then
K
induces an isomorphism on the cohomology.

We have the filtration

(4.33)

\displaystyle F^{p}CM^{\bullet}\coloneqq\left\{(c,\alpha)\mid|c|\geq p\right\}

to be the subcomplex generated by pairs whose component
c
has grading at least
p
.

In the closed case, Hutchings
[
30
]
proved that the corresponding spectral sequence agrees with the Leray–Serre spectral sequence from the
E_{2}
-page. In what follows, we adapt this argument to the case when the fiber is a cylindrical Lagrangian.

By our construction, the Morse–Smale pair
(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})
induces a CW-complex decomposition of
BG(N)
(cf. Remark
4.23
below). The unstable (descending) manifold
W^{u}(c)
of a critical point
c\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})
admits a compactification with faces, whose
k
-stratum is given by

\displaystyle\overline{W^{u}(c)_{k}}=\coprod_{I}\mathcal{M}_{I}\times W^{u}(r_{k}),

where

\mathcal{M}_{I}\coloneqq\coprod_{c=r_{0},r_{1},\cdots,r_{k}\text{ distinct}}\mathcal{M}(r_{0},r_{1})\times\cdots\mathcal{M}(r_{k-1},r_{k})

for each sequence of critical points
I=\{r_{0},r_{1},\cdots,r_{k}\}
. The evaluation map
e:\overline{W^{u}(c)}\rightarrow BG(N)
is smooth, and its restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{I}\times W^{u}(r_{k})
coincides with the projection onto
W^{u}(r_{k})
, while
e|_{W^{u}(c)}
is the identity. Each unstable manifold
W^{u}(c)
(resp. closure
\overline{W^{u}(c)}
) is homeomorphic to an open (resp. closed) disk of dimension
|c|
, with
e
serving as the characteristic map. As a result, every unstable manifold closure is contained in finitely many cells, satisfying the axioms of a CW-complex.

Remark 4.23
.

As shown in
[
38
,
58
]
, if a pseudo-gradient vector field agrees with the genuine gradient vector field of a quadratic form with respect to the Euclidean metric in a neighborhood of each critical point, then the unstable manifolds define a CW decomposition of the underlying manifold as illustrated above. Such assumptions are referred to as
special Morse functions
in
[
38
]
and as
locally trivial metrics
in
[
58
]
.

In our case, this assumption is automatic and is incorporated into Definition
3.5
. For the Morse functions
\mathrm{Re}\left(\operatorname{Tr}(A_{n}P_{V})\right)
on the complex Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}(k,N)
, this property can be verified directly from the explicit expression of the gradient flow in (
3.19
). In fact, the resulting CW decomposition coincides with the classical Schubert cell decomposition, as shown in Example
3.9
. When lifting a gradient vector field on the base to a pseudo-gradient vector field on the total space of a fibration, we always have sufficient flexibility in the choice of Morse functions and Riemannian metrics in the fiber direction.

The existence of CW-complex structure relies on the compactness of each
BG(N)
. When working with a general manifold
M
, the situation changes substantially. For a Morse-Smale pair
(f,\mathcal{V})
on
M
, let
M^{a}\coloneqq f^{-1}\left((-\infty,a]\right)
and
K^{a}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{f(p)\leq a}W^{u}(p)
. Assuming that
f
is bounded below and satisfies Palais–Smale condition (C), one only obtains a CW decomposition of the sets
K^{a}
, because the flow is only forward complete.

However, in our case, where
M=L
or its Borel space
L(N)
is a cylindrical Lagrangian, the Morse function is linear near infinity, and therefore proper and bounded below (also known as exhaustive). There is a homotopy equivalence between
K^{a}
and
M^{a}
(see, for example,
[
58
, Theorem 3.8]
). Moreover, the vector fields that appear in the family of Morse theory can also be realized as pseudo-gradient vector fields for suitable Morse functions. By
[
2
, Theorem 2.8]
, this implies a further homotopy equivalence
M^{a}\cong M
,
whenever
a
is taken larger than the maximal critical value of
f
.

Therefore, even though
L
is noncompact, it suffices to compare the Morse cohomology of
\widehat{f}_{N}
with the cellular cohomology of the underlying finite CW-complex, which is a standard identification. This reduction relies crucially on the fact that we only work with Liouville manifolds of
finite type
(in fact, only those obtained by completions of Liouville domains). The result may fail depending on one’s definition of Liouville manifolds.

Proposition 4.24

(Morse-theoretic Leray–Serre spectral sequence)
.

Let
(L(N),\widehat{f}_{N})
be defined as above, and let
\pi_{N}:L(N)\rightarrow BG(N)
denote the projection in the Borel construction. Let
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{p,q}
denote the spectral sequence associated to the filtration (
4.33
). Then the following hold:

(1)

The Morse-theoretic spectral sequence
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{p,q}
is isomorphic to the Leray–Serre spectral sequence
LS_{k}^{p,q}
for
k\geq 2
.

(2)

The family Morse cohomology
HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})
is isomorphic to the cellular cohomology of the total space, that is,

HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})\cong H_{\mathrm{Cell}}^{\bullet}(L(N)),

and hence to the singular cohomology
H^{\bullet}(L(N))
.

Proof.

Since each fiberwise Morse function
f_{c}
satisfies the Palais-Smale condition (C), the argument of
[
30
]
applies verbatim in our case. Here we give an alternative proof based on the CW-complex decomposition; a closely related approach can be found in
[
52
]
.

The vector field defining the moduli space (
4.32
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{L(N)}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\#}-\nabla^{v}f_{\gamma(\tau)},

where
\mathcal{V}_{N}^{\#}
denotes the horizontal lift of the negative pseudo-gradient vector field
\mathcal{V}_{N}
. We now show that this vector field can also be realized as the pseudo-gradient of a suitably constructed Morse function
F_{N}
.

Note that the argument of Lemma
3.6
does not apply directly, as each fiber is noncompact and we would need a uniform bound on the fiberwise Morse function
|f_{c}|
and its gradient
\left\lVert\nabla^{v}f_{c}\right\rVert
to ensure that it will not break the pseudo-gradient properties and no new critical points are generated. However, because each fiber
L
admits a cylindrical structure, we can borrow some ideas from Floer theory and resolve this issue in a soft manner.

As before, choose a subspace
L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)\subset L(N)
, proper over the base, that it contains all the critical points

\prod_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})}\operatorname{Crit}(f_{c_{i}}).

Outside a collared neighborhood of
L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, choose a smooth function
F_{\mathrm{out}}
such that
dF_{\mathrm{out}}(\mathcal{V}_{L_{N}})<0
. This is possible because each fiberwise function
f_{b}
is linear near infinity; indeed, any positive multiple of the radial coordinate
r
suffices. (Note that
r
is globally defined outside
L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
since it is
G
-invariant).

In the same notation as in Lemma
3.6
, we define a function on
L(N)
by

(4.34)

\displaystyle F_{N}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\pi_{N}^{*}f_{N}+\sum_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}f_{c_{i}}&in $L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)$\\
F_{\mathrm{out}}&outside a neighborhood of $L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)$\end{cases}

where the two definitions are interpolated by a smooth cutoff function on a collared neighborhood of
\partial L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, so that
F_{N}
is a smooth function.

Since each
\phi_{i}
is constant near the critical point
c_{i}
, for sufficiently small
\epsilon_{i}
,
F_{N}
is a Morse function and its critical points coincide with the generators of the complex
CM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})
. To verify the pseudo-gradient property, observe that inside
L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
we have

(4.35)

\displaystyle dF_{N}(\mathcal{V}_{L(N)})=df_{N}(\mathcal{V}_{N})-\sum_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})}\epsilon_{i}d\phi_{i}(\mathcal{V}_{N})f_{c_{i}}+\sum_{c_{i}\in\operatorname{Crit}(f_{N})}\epsilon_{i}\phi_{i}df_{c_{i}}(-\nabla^{v}f_{\gamma(\tau)}).

Since
\mathcal{V}_{N}
is a pseudo-gradient adapted to
f_{N}
, and the family
f_{b}
is locally constant around
c_{i}
, the first and third term are both non-positive. Inside
L^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, the second term is also bounded. The rest of the proof is identical to that in Lemma
3.6
.

As discussed above, there is a homotopy equivalence

\displaystyle K(N)^{a}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{F_{N}(p)\leq a}W^{u}(p)\cong L(N)^{a}\cong L(N),

when
a
is chosen larger than all critical values of
F_{N}
.

The resulting CW-complex has the property that the projection of each
k
-cell corresponds to a
k^{\prime}
-cell in the base, since by construction, the projection of the unstable manifold of
\mathcal{V}_{L(N)}
is precisely the unstable manifold of
\mathcal{V}_{N}
. Hence, the associated cellular complex

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cell}^{k}(K(N)^{a})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{K}\langle e_{i}^{k}\rangle

admits a natural filtration

\displaystyle F^{p}\mathrm{Cell}^{k}(K(N)^{a})=\bigoplus_{\dim\pi(e_{i}^{k})\geq p}\mathbb{K}\langle e_{i}^{k}\rangle,

and the corresponding spectral sequence is a cellular model for the Leray–Serre spectral sequence (see, for example,
[
47
]
). We remark that the compactness of the base

BG(N)
ensures that the unstable manifolds of
(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})
assemble into a finite CW–complex structure.

By the functoriality of the Leray–Serre spectral sequence and the homotopy equivalences established above, it therefore suffices to work with the Leray–Serre spectral sequence associated to the finite CW-complex
K(N)^{a}
.

We define a map from the Morse complex
CM^{\bullet}
to the cellular complex
\mathrm{Cell}^{k}(K(N)^{a})
by sending each generator
\mathbf{a}\coloneqq(c,\alpha)
to the corresponding unstable manifold, which can be regarded as a basis of the cellular cohomology. That this map is a chain morphism follows from the classical principle “Morse cohomology is the cellular cohomology”. We briefly recall the construction here (see, for example,
[
52
,
58
,
9
]
).

Let
\mathcal{B}^{k}
denote the
k
-skeleton of the CW-complex. Then the cellular cochain complex
\mathrm{Cell}^{k}(L(N))
is given by
H^{k}(\mathcal{B}^{k},\mathcal{B}^{k-1};\mathbb{K})
, with the differential
\partial_{\mathrm{Cell}}
defined as the connecting homomorphism in the long exact sequence

\displaystyle H^{k}(\mathcal{B}^{k},\mathcal{B}^{k-1};\mathbb{K})\rightarrow H^{k}(\mathcal{B}^{k};\mathbb{K})\rightarrow H^{k+1}(\mathcal{B}^{k+1},\mathcal{B}^{k};\mathbb{K}).

Explicitly, one has

(4.36)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathrm{Cell}}(e_{i}^{k})=\sum_{j}[e_{i}^{k}:e_{j}^{k+1}]e_{j}^{k+1}

where
[e_{i}^{k}:e_{j}^{k+1}]
denotes the incidence number.

These incidence numbers satisfy
[W^{u}(\mathbf{b}),W^{u}(\mathbf{a})]=\#\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N})
. Furthermore, by the construction of
(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})
(Definition
3.7
) and the assumption that each
f_{c_{i}}
is a Morse function that is linear near infinity, the right-hand side of (
4.36
) is a finite sum. This correspondence respects the filtration tautologically and induces an isomorphism of the cochain complex.

Therefore, the Morse spectral sequence coincides with the Leray–Serre spectral sequence from the
E_{2}
-page (all models of Leray–Serre spectral sequence are isomorphic from
E_{2}
-page).
∎

From Floer to Morse

Similarly we consider the spectral sequence
E_{\bullet}^{p,q}
associated with the filtration

\displaystyle F^{p}CF^{\bullet}(L,L;X(N))\coloneqq\{(c,x)\mid|c|\geq p\}.

To compare the spectral sequence
E_{\bullet}^{p,q}
with the Leray–Serre spectral sequence, by Proposition
4.24
it suffices to compare
E_{\bullet}^{p,q}
with the Morse-theoretic spectral sequence
\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}^{p,q}
associated with the filtration (
4.33
) for the family of Morse function
(L(N),\widehat{f}_{N})
.

Theorem 4.25

(Comparison with Leray–Serre)
.

Let
\pi_{N}:L(N)\rightarrow 0_{BG(N)}
be the fibration constructed before. Then:

(1)

There is an isomorphism from the
E_{2}
-page onward between the spectral sequence
E_{\bullet}^{p,q}
and the Morse-theoretic Leray–Serre spectral sequence
\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}^{p,q}
.

(2)

The Floer cohomology
HF^{\bullet}(L,L;X(N))
is isomorphic to the family Morse cohomology
HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})
.

Proof.

The proof follows from an adaptation of the standard pearl trajectory argument (see, for example,
[
6
,
10
]
) to the setting of a fibration, see Figure
1
. For generators of the Lagrangian Floer complex associated to the Morse function
f_{N}
, we write

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\coloneqq(c_{i},x)

\displaystyle\in CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N)),

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}\coloneqq(c_{j},\alpha)

\displaystyle\in CM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N}).

We define the moduli space

\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)

to consist of triples
(\gamma,\varphi,u)
, where
(\gamma,\varphi)
is a family of Morse trajectory and
u
is a pseudo-holomorphic strip satisfying the following conditions:

•

The family Morse trajectory is a map

\displaystyle\varphi:(-\infty,0]\longrightarrow L(N)

together with a projection
\gamma:(-\infty,0]\rightarrow BG(N)
, and
(\gamma,\varphi)
solves the fiberwise Morse equation (
4.32
) associated to the family
\widehat{f}_{N}
.

•

The pseudo-holomorphic component
u

\displaystyle u:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\rightarrow L(N)

is a strip mapping into
L(N)
, which also projects to
\gamma
, that satisfies the inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}u^{\mathrm{vert}}+J_{(\tau,t)}^{\mathrm{vert}}\left(\partial_{t}u^{\mathrm{vert}}-\psi(\tau)X_{H_{(\tau)}}\right)=0,

where
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
is a smooth cutoff function such that
\psi(\tau)=0
for
\tau\ll 0
and
\psi(\tau)=1
for
\tau\gg 0
.

Thus,
\psi(\tau)
interpolates between the Cauchy–Riemann equation
\overline{\partial}_{J}u=0
and the inhomogeneous Floer equation (
4.3
). They are required to satisfy following boundary and asymptotic conditions:

(4.37)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}u(\tau,i)\in L(N),&\tau\in\mathbb{R},i=0,1;\\
\lim_{\tau\rightarrow+\infty}u(\tau,t)=\mathbf{x};\\
\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}u(\tau,t)=\varphi(0);\\
\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(\gamma,\varphi)(\tau)=\mathbf{a}.\end{cases}

Figure 1.
Configurations of pearl trajectories that define the maps
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}
and
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}^{-1}
.

Up to generic choices,
\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)
is a smooth manifold.
By our choice of Hamiltonians, we have
E(u)<\infty
, and hence Gromov-Floer compactness applies. Consequently, we can compactify the one-dimensional stratum of
\mathcal{P}\bigl(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\bigr)
by adding either a Morse trajectory or a Floer strip. The boundary strata admit the explicit description

(4.38)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{[1]}\overline{\mathcal{P}}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)=&\bigcup_{\mathbf{b}\in CM^{\bullet}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N})\times\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)\\
&\cup\bigcup_{\mathbf{y}\in CF^{\bullet}}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b};\widehat{f}_{N}).\end{split}

Now define
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}:CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{1};X(N))\rightarrow CM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{PSS}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}=(c_{i},\alpha)\\
|\mathbf{a}|=|\mathbf{x}|\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)\mathbf{a}.

It follows from (
4.38
) that
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}
is a chain map. To see that
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}
respects the filtration, note that there is a natural projection
\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(c_{i},c_{j};f_{N})
which implies that whenever

\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{x};H(N),\widehat{f}_{N}\right)\neq\emptyset
,
we have
|c_{i}|\geq|c_{j}|
, since
(f_{N},\mathcal{V}_{N})
is Morse-Smale. On the
E_{2}
-page, it induces a morphism between the spectral sequence

(4.39)

\displaystyle E_{2}^{p,q}\xrightarrow{[\mathrm{PSS}_{N}]}\mathcal{E}_{\bullet}^{p,q}.

The inverse morphism
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}^{-1}
is defined similarly:
the pearl trajectory now starts from a Hamiltonian chord and connects to a Morse critical point via a negative gradient flow line.
In this case, the half–disk solves the Floer equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}u^{\mathrm{vert}}+J_{(\tau,t)}^{\mathrm{vert}}\left(\partial_{t}u^{\mathrm{vert}}-\psi(-\tau)X_{H_{(\tau)}}\right)=0,

To conclude the proof, we apply a standard gluing and homotopy argument (see
[
6
]
) to show the compositions
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{PSS}_{N}
and
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}\circ\mathrm{PSS}_{N}^{-1}
are homotopic to the identity respectively.

One convenient way to reduce the fibration setting to the classical case is to trivialize the pearl trajectory over the Morse trajectory of
f_{N}
.
Recall that the connection induced by the symplectic form
\omega_{X(N)}
defines a parallel transport that preserves the Lagrangian submanifolds (see Section
3.2
).
Since
L
is exact, we conclude that (
4.39
) is an isomorphism, with inverse given by
\mathrm{PSS}_{N}^{-1}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.20
.

Similar to the argument of Lemma
4.12
, the family Morse cohomology
HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})
forms an inverse system under the projection maps
P_{N}
induced by the inclusions.

In view of the proof of Theorem
4.25
, an argument parallel to Lemma
4.12
gives rise to the following commutative diagram:

(4.40)

Taking the inverse limit over
N
, we obtain

HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L)\cong\varprojlim_{N}HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N}).

Thus it remains to verify the isomorphisms

HM^{\bullet}(L(N);\widehat{f}_{N})\cong H^{\bullet}(L(N))

are functorial with respect to the approximation.

Recall that in the proof of Proposition
4.24
, this isomorphism was constructed by passing through the cellular cohomology of the CW-complex structure associated to
\widehat{f}_{N}
. The inclusion
L(N)\hookrightarrow L(N+1)
induces a morphism
H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Cell}}(L(N+1))\rightarrow H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Cell}}(L(N))
, and the following diagram commutes

(4.41)
