This commutativity is tautological, since the cellular differential (
4.36
) is exactly the Morse differential by construction. Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L)\cong\varprojlim_{N}H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Cell}}(L(N))\cong H_{\mathrm{Cell}}^{\bullet}(L_{G};\mathbb{K})\cong H_{G}^{\bullet}(L;\mathbb{K}).

The last two isomorphisms are standard.
∎

5.
Equivariant Fukaya categories

5.1.
A
G
-equivariant directed category

We shall now extend the discussion from the previous section to an
A_{\infty}
-category. The construction follows an approach analogous to the
fiberwise wrapped Fukaya category
developed by Abouzaid and Auroux
[
3
]
. However, due to the structure provided by the Borel–Liouville construction, our situation is technically more accessible than in
loc. cit.

To proceed, we reformulate part of the setup from the previous section in a more compact form for later reference.

Lemma-Definition 5.1

(cf.
[
3
]
)
.

Let
X(N)\xrightarrow{\pi_{N}}T^{*}BG(N)
be the Borel–Liouville construction of a manifold. Fixed a closed subspace
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)\subset X(N)
which is proper over the base. Then one can choose a triple
(H,h,J_{N})
consisting of:

(1)

an almost complex structure
J_{N}
on
X(N)
, such that its restriction to each fiber is compatible with the symplectic form, and
\pi_{N}
is
J_{N}
-holomorphic;

(2)

a
J_{N}
-plurisubharmonic function
h:X(N)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
;

(3)

a non-negative Hamiltonian
H:X(N)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
called the wrapping Hamiltonian.

These data are required to satisfy:

(4)

The restrictions of
h
and
H
to each fiber of
\pi_{N}
are proper. The intersection of
X^{\mathrm{in}}
with any fiber is a sublevel set of
h
.

(5)

The Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
is vertical, i.e.
d\pi_{N}(X_{H})=0
. The parallel transport preserves the level sets of
h
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, i.e.

dh(X_{H})=0\quad\text{ outside }X^{\mathrm{in}}(N).

(6)

If
\xi^{\#}
is the horizontal lift of a vector on
T^{*}BG(N)
, then
dH(\xi^{\#})=0
, and

\displaystyle dh(\xi^{\#})=0,\quad d^{c}h(\xi^{\#})=0\quad\text{outside }X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)

where
d^{c}h\coloneqq-dh\circ J_{N}
.

(7)

The
1
-form
d^{c}h
is invariant under both flows near infinity:
\mathcal{L}_{X_{H}}d^{c}h=\mathcal{L}_{\xi^{\#}}d^{c}h=0
away from
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
.

(8)

(Non-negative wrapping)
d^{c}h(X_{H})\geq 0
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
.

Finally, the choices
(H,h,J_{N})
are required to be compatible with the approximation as
N
varies.

Remark 5.2
.

When
X
is a Liouville sector, in addition to the requirements of Lemma
5.1
, we also impose a boundary compatibility condition. Namely, near
\pi_{\partial X}
the product decomposition induces a projection

\left(\mathrm{Nbh}(\partial X),J_{N}\right)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{|Re|\geq 0}},j),

which we require to be
(J,j)
-holomorphic. In the Borel construction, by a standard partition of unity argument, this extends to a projection

\displaystyle\pi_{\partial X(N)}:\mathrm{Nbh}(\partial X(N))\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{|Re|\geq 0}}.

As shown in
[
19
]
, there is abundant freedom in the choice of almost complex structures so that the vertical holomorphicity condition

\displaystyle d\pi_{\partial X(N)}|_{TX(N)^{\mathrm{vert}}}\circ J_{N}=j\circ d\pi_{\partial X(N)}|_{TX(N)^{\mathrm{vert}}}

is satisfied. This ensures that the standard maximum principal applies, thereby preventing the pseudo-holomorphic strips from escaping cross the boundary.

To construct a wrapping Hamiltonian
H
, we require it to be compatible with the
G
-action, in the sense that
\mathcal{L}_{X_{\xi}}X_{H}=0
for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. In the case where
X
is a Liouville manifold, such a Hamiltonian can be constructed in a straightforward manner: one begins with a proper linear Hamiltonian function and and averages it over the group
G
. The following lemma shows that a similar construction extends to Liouville sectors, and moreover produces a cofinal wrapping sequence of Lagrangians.

Lemma 5.3

(
G
-invariant wrapping)
.

Let
X
be a Liouville sector with compact contact boundary
\partial_{\infty}X
. Then there exists a
G
-invariant, non-negative Hamiltonian function

H:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

such that:

(1)

H
is a
G
-invariant linear Hamiltonian that vanishes to second order precisely on
\partial X
;

(2)

the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}|_{X^{\circ}}
is complete;

(3)

the action of
G
preserves the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
;

(4)

the Hamiltonian flow lies in the levels sets of
\mu_{X}
.

Proof.

We begin by choosing a proper nonnegative function that is linear on the cylindrical end and vanishes to second order along
\partial X
. Average this function over
G
, we obtain a nonnegative Hamiltonian function
H
.

For property (1), note that because the
G
-action is a local diffeomorphism, it preserves the boundary
\partial X
. Since
G
preserves the Liouville structure, it also preserves the radial coordinate; hence
H
is a
G
-invariant linear Hamiltonian that vanishes precisely on
\partial X
.

By construction,
H|_{X^{\circ}}
is strictly positive and proper. Furthermore, since
dH(X_{H})=0
, the Hamiltonian flow preserves the level sets of
H
. In particular, the vector field
X_{H}
cannot drive points away from compact sets in finite time. Escape lemma then implies that
X_{H}
is a complete vector field on
X^{\circ}
, establishing (2).

To verify (3) and (4), let
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and denote by
X_{\xi}
the infinitesimal vector field. Taking the Lie derivative of defining relation
\omega(X_{H},\cdot)=-dH
, we obtain
\mathcal{L}_{X_{\xi}}X_{H}=0
. Let
\phi_{H}^{t}
denote the Hamiltonian flow of
X_{H}
. Recall that the moment map of a Liouville
G
-action is given by
\langle\mu_{X}(x),\xi\rangle=\lambda(X_{\xi})
(See Proposition
2.7
).
We compute for
x\in X
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
:

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{X}(\phi^{t}_{H}(x)),\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda\left(X_{\xi}(\phi^{t}_{H}(x))\right)

\displaystyle=((\phi^{t}_{H})^{*}\lambda)(X_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\lambda(X_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\langle\mu_{X}(x),\xi\rangle,

where the second equality follows from the commutation
[X_{H},X_{\xi}]=0
established in (3).
Thus we have shown that
\mu_{X}
is invariant under the Hamiltonian flow of
X_{H}
. Clearly, the same argument applies verbatim in the Liouville manifold case by taking
\partial X=\emptyset
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

The family of almost complex structures has already been constructaed in Section
4.1
. Choose a
J_{N}
-plurisubharmonic function
h:X(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which agrees with the radial coordinate
r
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
. Let
H
be a G-invariant non-negative function chosen as in Lemma
5.3
; it desecnds to a Hamiltonian function on
X(N)
through the quotient map

(5.1)

\displaystyle\left(X\times T^{*}EG(N),d\lambda_{X}+d\lambda_{\mathrm{can}}\right)\longrightarrow(X(N),d\lambda_{X(N)}).

The verification of the conditions in Lemma
5.1
proceeds as follows.

Condition (4) holds since both
H
and
h
are linear near infinity. The projection
\pi_{N}
is induced by the projection to the second factor in (
5.1
), so that
d\pi_{N}(X_{H})=0
. By construction
h
agrees with
r
outside
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, and
r
is invariant under the parallel transport away from
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, hence Condition (5) follows. To see (6), recall that the connection we use is induced by the symplectic form
\omega_{X(N)}
so that

dH(\xi^{\#})=-\omega_{X(N)}(X_{H},\xi^{\#})=0.

The same results apllies to
dh
when restricted away from
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
. Finally, both conditions (7) and (8) follow immediately from the assumption that
J_{N}
is fiberwise of contact type near infinity.

∎

Let us fix a countable collection of exact cylindrical
G
-invariant Lagrangians

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}\coloneqq\{L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},\ldots\},

which we refer to as
admissible Lagrangians
.
Applying the Liouville Borel construction to these objects yields a corresponding countable collection of Borel Lagrangian spaces

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{N}\coloneqq\{L_{0}(N),L_{1}(N),L_{2}(N),\ldots\}.

In analogy with the construction of equivariant Floer cohomology, our goal is to associate to each
N\in\mathbb{N}
an
A_{\infty}
-category, and to show that the approximation functors arising from varying the level
N
are compatible with the
A_{\infty}
-structures.

Trees and Disks

To this end, we study a hybrid object consisting of Morse trees and pseudo-holomorphic disks that solve fiberwise Cauchy-Riemann equations with inhomogeneous perturbation terms. As before, each such disk lies over the corresponding tree.

We begin by recalling the following definition.

Definition 5.4
.

A
directed
d
-leafed planar tree

T
is a driected tree embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
consisting of the following data:

(1)

a set of vertices
V(T)
and a set of edges
E(T)
;

(2)

a collection of
d
semi-infinite outgoing edges, referred to as leaves;

(3)

a distinguished vertex
v_{0}\in V(T)
, called the
root
, which is connected to a single semi-infinite incoming edge;

(4)

a decomposition
E(T)=E^{\mathrm{ext}}(T)\sqcup E^{\mathrm{int}}(T)
, where
E^{\mathrm{ext}}(T)
consists of semi-infinite (external) edges, and
E^{\mathrm{int}}(T)
consists of the (internal) internal edges;

For a vertex
v\in V(T)
, we denote by
|v|
its valency. The tree
T
is called

stable
if
|v|\geq 3
for all
v
, and
semistable
if
|v|\geq 2
.

Let
\mathbf{L}_{T}\subset\mathbf{L}
be a finite subset of admissible Lagrangians. We say that a tree
T
is
labeled by

\mathbf{L}_{T}
if each connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus T
assigned a label from
\mathbf{L}_{T}
.

We fix an orientation so that every edge is directed from root to the leaves. This determines functions

\mathrm{lef},\mathrm{rig}:E(T)\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{T}

such that for each edge
e\in E(T)
, the label
\mathrm{lef}(e)
(resp.
\mathrm{rig}(e)
) denotes the Lagrangian lying to the left (resp. right) of
e\in E(T)
with respect to its orientation.

A Riemannian metric
g_{T}
on
T
assigns a length to each edge of
T
such that any external edge is of infinite length.

For each pair of Lagrangian
L_{i},L_{j}\in\mathbf{L}
, we assign an admissible family of Morse-Smale pairs

(f_{(i,j),N},\mathcal{V}_{(i,j),N})

where
f_{(i,j),N}:BG(N)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a Morse function and
\mathcal{V}_{(i,j),N}
is the corresponding pseudo-gradient vector field of
f_{(i,j),N}
in the sense of Theorem-Definition
3.7
.

By Lemma
4.6
, such data can be chosen inductively. The corresponding
G
-equivariant Floer complex
CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{i_{0}},L_{i_{1}})
is then defined using the associated admissible Morse-Smale pairs
(f_{(i_{0},i_{1}),N},\mathcal{V}_{(i_{0},i_{1}),N})
following the construction as in Section
4
.

In some cases, such as when defining a product structure on

\mathbf{x}_{i}\coloneqq(c_{i},x_{i})\in CF^{\bullet}(L_{0},L_{0};X(N)),

the moduli space involves counting intersections of stable manifolds of critical points
c_{i}
and
c_{j}
of
f_{(0,0)}
. These intersections are empty unless
c_{i}=c_{j}
. To achieve the transversality in such situations, it is therefore necessary to introduce appropriate perturbation data. We follow the construction as in
[
5
, Definition 2.6]
.

Definition 5.5
.

A
gradient flow perturbation datum
on a metric tree
(T,g_{T})
consists of an assignment, for each edge
e\in E(T)
, of a family of vector fields:

\displaystyle X_{e}(N):e\rightarrow T^{\infty}(BG(N))

which vanishes outside a bounded subset of
e
.

These data are chosen coherently in two directions: first, they are compatible with perturbations of the gradient flow equation on higher-dimensional moduli spaces of trees; second, they are compatible with the system of approximations as
N
varies.

Definition 5.6

(Horizontal Morse trees)
.

A (perturbed) Morse tree corresponding to
(T,g_{T})
with labels
L_{T}
is a continuous map

\displaystyle\psi:T\rightarrow BG(N)

whose restriction to every edge
e\in E(T)
solves perturbed gradient flow equation

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\psi|_{e}=\mathcal{V}_{(\mathrm{lef}(e),\mathrm{rig}(e)),N}+X_{e}(N).

The vertical component of the construction proceeds as in the standard setting, except that it now solves the pseudo-holomorphic equation fiberwise. We recall some notions following
[
63
,
4
]
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{d+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
denote universal family of disks with
d+1
ordered boundary marked points. The base
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,T}
can be compactified to a manifold with corners by adjoining all stable broken disks,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}=\coprod_{T}\mathcal{R}^{d+1,T}.

where each stratum
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,T}
denote the moduli space of broken disk modeled on
T
.

Let

\Sigma=\mathbb{D}\setminus\{z_{0},\cdots,z_{d}\}

denote a disk with
d+1
boundary marked points removed. For each puncture
z_{i}
, we choose a strip-like end
\epsilon_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\Sigma
in a neighborhood of
z_{i}
for all surfaces in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,T}
.

Such a family of strip-like ends is required to be chosen in a
universal way
in the sense of
[
63
]
, meaning that it is compatible with gluing along collar neighborhoods of boundary strata:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{i+1,T_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{j+1,T_{2}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{i+j+1,T}.

We denote by
\partial_{i}\Sigma
the boundary component between
z_{i}
and
z_{i+1}
, with the convention
z_{d+1}=z_{0}
.

Similarly, we choose a
universal perturbation datum
, namely a
1
-form
Y\otimes\alpha
on
\partial\Sigma
with values in vector fields. In our case, we consider moving boundary condition given by the flow of the
G
-invariant wrapping Hamiltonian
H
.

Fix a sub-closed
1
-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
satisfying

(5.2)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}^{*}\alpha=w_{i}dt\text{ for some }w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\quad d\alpha=0\text{ near }\partial\Sigma,\quad\alpha|_{\partial\Sigma}=0.

Definition 5.7

(Fiberwise pseudoholomorphic treed disks)
.

For a tree labeled by
\mathbf{L}_{T}
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{0};\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdots,\mathbf{x}_{d};X(N))
consists of pairs of maps
(\psi,u)
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

The map
\psi:T\rightarrow BG(N)
is the Morse tree defined in Definition
5.6
.

(2)

The map
u:\Sigma\rightarrow X(N)
solves the inhomogeneous fiberwise pseudoholomorphic holomorphic equation

(5.3)

\displaystyle(du^{\mathrm{vert}}-X_{H}\otimes\alpha)^{0,1}=0.

(3)

The projection
\pi_{N}\circ u=\psi
sends the thin parts of the disks to the edges of the tree, and thick parts to vertices.

(4)

(Boundary and asymptotic condition) Theses maps satisfy the boundary and asymptotic conditions:

\displaystyle u(\partial_{i}\Sigma)\subset L_{i}(N)\quad\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\pm\infty}(\psi,u)(\epsilon_{i}(\tau,\cdot))=\mathbf{x}_{i},

for generators
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in CF^{\bullet}(L_{i-1},L_{i};X(N))
.

By construction, the wrapping Hamiltonian
H
typically does not produce transverse intersections, since the intersection points occur in
G
-orbits. To remedy this issue, one supplements (
5.3
) with an additional compactly supported vertical inhomogeneous perturbation term. This local perturbation breaks the
G
-symmetry near the relevant intersection loci and achieve transversality.

After a generic choice of the perturbation data, the moduli space is a regular manifold of the expected dimension

\displaystyle d-2+|\mathbf{x}_{0}|-\sum_{1\leq k\leq d}|\mathbf{x}_{k}|.

This follows from the surjectivity of the linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator, which decomposes into vertical and horizontal components, each known to be surjective under generic perturbations. The surjectivity for the horizontal Morse tree component is established in
[
5
]
.

Proposition 5.8

(Maximum principle)
.

Let
u:\Sigma\to X(N)
be a solution to the inhomogeneous Floer equation (
5.3
).
Then
u
satisfies the following maximum principles:
The function
h\circ u
attains no local maxima outside of
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
, where
h
is the
J_{N}
-plurisubharmonic function defined in Lemma
5.1
.

Sketch of proof.

The statement follows from the standard maximum principle for
J_{N}
-holomorphic maps with inhomogeneous perturbations, applied to almost complex structures of contact type near infinity. One may either apply the general global version of the maximum principle
[
3
, Proposition 3.11]
, or give a direct argument exploiting the fact that we are working with a Liouville–Borel construction as follows.

Since the radial coordinate on the cylindrical end
r
is invariant under
G
-action, it is globally defined function away from
X^{\mathrm{in}}(N)
. Lemma
4.11
ensures that no pseudo-holomorphic strip escapes to infinity.
∎

Remark 5.9

(The product and
H_{G}^{\bullet}(*)
-module structure)
.

The product structure of
HW^{\bullet}
is defined by considering the tree with three edges. In particular, this induces a natural
H_{G}^{\bullet}(*)
-module structure on
HW^{\bullet}
.
Recall that the terminal map
L\rightarrow*
induces a morphism

H^{\bullet}_{G}(*)\rightarrow H^{\bullet}_{G}(L),

endowing
H^{\bullet}_{G}(L)
with the structure of a module over
H^{\bullet}_{G}(*)
.
By combining this map with the
G
-equviariant PSS isomorphism, the
\mu^{2}
product operation, and then passing to the direct limit, we obtain the composition

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}_{G}(*)\rightarrow H_{G}^{\bullet}(L)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{PSS}_{G}}HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L,L)\xrightarrow{\mu^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)}HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L,K)\rightarrow HW_{G}^{\bullet}(L,K),

which equips
HW^{\bullet}_{G}
with the desired
H^{\bullet}_{G}(*)
-module structure.

5.2.
G
-equivariant wrapped Fukaya category

For each object
L(N)\in\mathbf{L}_{N}
, we write

\displaystyle L(N)(t)\coloneqq\phi_{H}^{t}(L(N))

where
\phi_{H}^{t}
denotes the flow of the wrapping Hamiltonian
H
. By Condition (4) of Lemma
5.3
, the
H
-wrapping preserves the moment map level, so
L(t)
remains in the same level set of the moment map, and the same is true for all of its associated Borel spaces. Condition (6) of Lemma
5.1
implies that the wrapping isotopy commutes with the isotopy generated by the horizontal perturbation data.

Choose a generic
\epsilon>0
such that for every pair of Lagrangians
L_{0},L_{1}\in\mathbf{L}
, the intersection

L_{0}(N)(\epsilon k_{0})\cap L_{1}(N)(\epsilon k_{1})

is compact for all distinct integers
k_{0}\neq k_{1}
and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. This set of such admissible
\epsilon
is a countable intersection of Baire sets and hence is dense as well.

At each level
N
, we define a directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{O}_{N}
whose objects are
L^{k}(N)\coloneqq L(N)(-\epsilon k)
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
L\in\mathbf{L}
. The morphism spaces are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k_{0}},L_{1}^{k_{1}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}CF^{\bullet}\left(L_{0}(N)(-\epsilon k_{0}),L_{1}(N)(-\epsilon k_{1})\right)&if $k_{0}<k_{1}$,\\
\mathbb{K}\cdot\mathrm{id}&if $k_{0}=k_{1}$ and $L_{0}=L_{1}$,\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

The
A_{\infty}
-structures
\mu^{d}_{N}
are defined by counting the solutions to the inhomogeneous Floer equation (
5.3
) with compactly supported vertical perturbation terms. The perturbation data are chosen coherently across all moduli space (see
[
63
]
for details). Compactness of the resulting moduli space follows from the maximum principal as in Proposition
5.8
.

Equivariant Fukaya–Seidel category

Let
(I,\leq)
be a partially ordered set, viewed as a category in which there exists a unique morphism
i\to j
whenever
i\leq j
, and
i\to i
is the identity morphism.
Suppose we are given a strictly commuting diagram of
A_{\infty}
-categories

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{C}_{i},f_{ij}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{j}\}_{i\leq j\in I},

in the sense that for any composable sequence
[i\to j\to k]
in
I
, one has

\displaystyle f_{jk}\circ f_{ij}=f_{ik}.

Here each
f_{ij}
is assumed to be a strict
A_{\infty}
-functor.

Definition 5.10
.

The
inverse limit

A_{\infty}
-category

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{i}

is defined as follows:

(1)

An object
C
of
\mathcal{C}
is a family

\displaystyle\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C}_{i})

satisfying
f_{ij}(C_{i})=C_{j}
for all
i\leq j
.

(2)

For objects
C^{k_{0}},C^{k_{1}}\in\mathcal{C}
, the morphism space is given by the inverse limit

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(C^{k_{0}},C^{k_{1}})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{i}(C^{k_{0}}_{i},C^{k_{1}}_{i}).

(3)

The
A_{\infty}
-operations are defined componentwise:

\displaystyle\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{C}}(c^{k_{1}},\ldots,c^{k_{d}})\coloneqq\varprojlim_{i\in I}\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}(c^{k_{1}}_{i},\ldots,c^{k_{d}}_{i})

for morphisms
c^{k_{j}}\in\mathcal{C}(C^{k_{j}},C^{k_{j+1}})
. Since each
\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}^{d}
satisfies the
A_{\infty}
-relations, so does
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

We now show that the projection map induces a strict
A_{\infty}
-functor.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
P_{N}
denote the projection map introduced in Section
4.4
. Then
P_{N}
induces a strict
A_{\infty}
-functor, that is

(5.4)

\displaystyle P_{N+1}\left(\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{O}_{N+1}}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\cdots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{k}})\right)=\mu_{\mathcal{O}_{N}}^{d}\left(P_{N+1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\cdots,P_{N+1}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}})\right),

for all
\mathbf{x}_{i_{k}}\in CF^{\bullet}(L_{i_{k-1}},L_{i_{k}};X(N+1))
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to that of Lemma
4.12
, with the role of Morse trajectories replaced by Morse trees. Since
\psi=\pi_{N+1}\circ u
solves the Morse tree equations,
we obtain, whenever,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{0}};\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}};X(N+1))\neq\emptyset
, a fibration of moduli spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{[T]}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{0}};\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}};X(N+1))\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{0}};\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}};X(N+1))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\pi_{N+1}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}(c_{i_{0}};c_{i_{1}}\cdots,c_{i_{d}};X(N+1)),

where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Morse}}
denotes the moduli space of Morse trees defining the
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure of
0_{BG_{N+1}}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{[T]}
denote the subspace of
\mathcal{M}
projecting to a fixed tree
T
.

By the construction of admissible families of Morse functions (Definition
3.7
), the counts of such configurations remain invariant under inclusion. As a result, the equality (
5.4
) follows.
∎

As a corollary, let
I\coloneqq(\mathbb{N},\leq)
be the poset of natural numbers, then the collection
\{\mathcal{O}_{i}\}_{i\in I^{\operatorname{op}}}
of Fukaya–Seidel categories forms an inverse system, where the morphisms are given by the projection functors

P_{i+1}:\mathcal{O}_{i+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{i}
.

Definition 5.12
.

We define the
equivariant directed category
as the inverse limit

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{G}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{i\in I^{\operatorname{op}}}\mathcal{O}_{i}.

An object of
\mathcal{O}_{G}
associated to a Lagrangian
L_{k}\in\mathbf{L}
is given by a compatible sequence of Borel Lagrangians. For notational convenience, we continue to denote such sequences simply by
L_{k}
. The morphism space are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{G}(L_{0}^{k_{0}},L_{1}^{k_{1}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}CF_{G}^{\bullet}\left(L_{0}(-\epsilon k_{0}),L_{1}(-\epsilon k_{1})\right)&if $k_{0}<k_{1}$,\\
\mathbb{K}\cdot\mathrm{id}&if $k_{0}=k_{1}$ and $L_{0}=L_{1}$,\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

The
A_{\infty}
-operations are defined componentwise, as in Definition
5.10
.

A standard construction
[
19
,
3
]
produces a distinguished collection of elements

\displaystyle\{e_{L^{k}}\}\subset HF^{0}(L^{k},L^{k+1}),

called the
quasi-units
, defined as the images of the identity class in
H^{\bullet}(L^{k})
under the composition

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}(L^{k})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{PSS}}HF^{\bullet}(L^{k},L^{k})\longrightarrow HF^{\bullet}(L^{k},L^{k+1}).

Analogously, we define the
G
-equivariant quasi-units
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{G}
denote the set of representatives of elements in
HF_{G}^{0}(L^{k},L^{k+1})
obtained as the image of the identity class under the composition

\displaystyle H_{G}^{\bullet}(L^{k})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{PSS}_{G}}HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L^{k},L^{k})\longrightarrow HF_{G}^{\bullet}(L^{k},L^{k+1}).

Here, the continuation maps and
G
-equivariant PSS isomorphisms are established in Sections
4.5
and
4.6
respectively.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
(X,\lambda)
be a Liouville manifold acted by a compact connected Lie group
G
which preserves the Liouville structure. The
G
-equivariant wrapped Fukaya category is defined as the localization of the equivariant directed category
\mathcal{O}_{G}
by the quasi-units

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)\coloneqq\mathcal{Z}_{G}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{G}

in the sense of
[
42
]
.

Following
[
3
]
, we now present an explicit model of the
G
-equivariant wrapped Fukaya category, which will be convenient for subsequent computations. We begin by constructing explicit cochain-level representatives of the
G
-equivariant quasi-units.

At each level
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the quasi-units are defined by counting maps
u:\mathbb{D}\setminus\{1\}\rightarrow X(N)
solving the Floer equation (
5.3
). Here,
\mathbb{D}\setminus\{1\}
is a punctured Riemann disk, and the moving boundary condition is given by
L^{t}=L(-\epsilon t)
for
t\in[k,k+1]
, constant near the end. We denote by

\displaystyle e_{L^{k}}^{N}\in CF^{0}\left(L(N)(-\epsilon k),L(N)(-\epsilon(k+1))\right)=\mathcal{O}_{N}(L^{k},L^{k+1}),

the cochain-level quasi-unit.
By choosing the perturbation data coherently with respect to the system of approximations, a similar argument as before shows that

(5.5)

\displaystyle P_{N+1}(e_{L^{k}}^{N+1})=e_{L^{k}}^{N}.

We therefore define

\displaystyle e^{G}_{L^{k}}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{N}e_{L^{k}}^{N}\in CF^{0}_{G}(L^{k},L^{k+1}),

and refer to it as the
G
-equivariant quasi-unit
.
Although the construction of quasi-units depends on several auxiliary choices, standard homotopy arguments ensure that the resulting elements are well-defined up to homotopy equivalence.

On the other hand, we have continuation maps as defined in Section
4.5
,

\displaystyle F^{N}_{L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j}}:\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j+1})

obtained by counting maps
u:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\rightarrow X(N)
that solve the Floer equation with corresponding moving boundary conditions. It is standard that composition with quasi-units is homotopic to the corresponding continuation map. We now adapt this construction to the setting of Borel construction.

More precisely, we stack the diagram of quasi-unit compositions over one another via the projections induced by the Borel construction (see Diagram
2
) and we verify that all such compositions commute up to homotopy.
Note that we adopt a different
A_{\infty}
-order convention from that used in
[
3
]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N+1}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N+1}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N+1}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N+1}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{N}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{N+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{N}\bigl(\cdot\text{, }e^{N}_{L_{1}^{j}}\bigr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{N}\bigl(e^{N}_{L_{0}^{k}}\text{, }\cdot\bigr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{N}\bigl(e^{N}_{L_{0}^{k}}\text{, }\cdot\bigr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{N}_{L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{N}\bigl(\cdot\text{, }e^{N}_{L_{1}^{j}}\bigr)}

Figure 2.
Quasi-units are natural with respect to continuation maps and approximations.

Proposition 5.14
.

The horizontal squares and triangles in Diagram
2
commute up to homotopy, while the vertical squares commute strictly.

Proof.

The homotopy commutativity of the horizontal squares and triangles follows from an argument identitical to that in
[
3
, Lemma 3.19]
.
The strict commutativity of the vertical squares is an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.11
together with the identification (
5.5
).
∎

By taking the inverse limit, we obtain the corresponding commutative diagram in
\mathcal{O}_{G}
.

Corollary 5.15
.

The following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

(5.6)

To proceed, for
L^{k}\in\mathcal{O}_{G}
, we denote by
\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k}}
the corresponding Yoneda module

\displaystyle X\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{G}(X,L^{k}).

By Corollary
5.15
, these Yoneda modules form a direct system, where the connecting morphisms
\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k+1}}
are induced by composition with
G
-equivariant quasi-units
e_{L^{k}}^{G}
.

Hence, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{L^{\infty}}\coloneqq\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k}},

where we model the homotopy colimit by the usual telescope construction:

\displaystyle\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k}}\cong\mathrm{Cone}\left(\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{Y}_{L^{k}}\right).

Here, the arrow denotes the direct sum of the maps
\mathrm{id}-e_{L^{k}}^{G}
, where
e_{L^{k}}^{G}
are the
G
-equivariant quasi-units.

Corollary
5.17
essentially means that the category generated by Yoneda modules
\mathcal{Y}_{L^{\infty}}
are
\mathcal{Z}_{G}
-local (see
[
19
, Section 3]
and
[
3
, Section 3.5]
), and therefore quasi-equivalent to the localization of
\mathcal{O}_{G}
. In other words, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{G}(L_{0},L_{1})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{Y}_{L_{0}^{\infty}},\mathcal{Y}_{L_{1}^{\infty}}).

For our purposes, we record only the minimal properties that will be used below, and refer the reader to the references above for a complete discussion.

Lemma 5.16
.

There is a quasi-isomorphism

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{Y}_{L_{0}^{\infty}},\mathcal{Y}_{L_{1}^{\infty}})\cong\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{holim}}\hskip 1.5pt\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{O}_{G}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j}),

where the morphisms in the direct and inverse system are given by composition with the
G
-equivariant quasi-units.

Proof.

Since the hom-functor preserves the limits, we obtain a natural duality between the the homotopy equalizer and coequalizer of the identity map and the connecting morphism.
These constructions provide respective models for homotopy (co)limit. See
[
3
, Lemma 3.22]
for further details.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

For each pair of objects
L_{0}^{k}
and
L_{1}
of objects in
\mathcal{O}
, there is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{j\rightarrow\infty}HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L^{k}_{0},L_{1}^{j})=H^{\bullet}\hom_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{Y}_{L_{0}^{\infty}},\mathcal{Y}_{L_{1}^{\infty}}).

Proof.

The map

\displaystyle\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{O}_{G}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j})\rightarrow\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{O}_{G}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j})

given by composing the qusi-unit is a quasi-isomorphism. This follows from the compatibility of the continuation maps with
G
-equivariant quasi-unit (Corollary
5.15
); see
[
3
, Lemma 3.23, Corollary 3.24]
for details. The inverse system obtained by varying
k
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition. In particular, the following composition

(5.7)

\displaystyle\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{O}_{G}(L_{0}^{k+1},L_{1}^{j})\leftarrow\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{holim}}\hskip 1.5pt\underset{j\rightarrow\infty}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\hskip 1.5pt\mathcal{O}_{G}(L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j})\cong\hom_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{Y}_{L_{0}^{\infty}},\mathcal{Y}_{L_{1}^{\infty}})

induces an isomorphism on cohomology.
∎

6.
Equivariant wrapped Floer theory for Lagrangian correspondences

Throughout this section, we restrict to the case where the Liouville action of
G
on a Liouville manifold
X
is free. For simplicity of exposition, we will work with ungraded (wrapped) Floer groups.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)_{0}\subset\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)
denote the full subcategory generated by
G
-invariant exact Lagrangians contained in
\mu^{-1}(0)
, we construct a
(\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)_{0},\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G))
-bilinear module

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}:\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}_{0}\times\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)\rightarrow\mathbf{Ch},

which on the level of cohomology takes
L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}
to isomorphic cohomology groups
HW^{\bullet}_{G}(L_{i},L_{j})=HW^{\bullet}(\underline{L}_{i},\underline{L}_{j})
. We then show for
L\in\operatorname{Ob}\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)_{0}
, the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}(L,-):\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)_{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)}

takes values in representable right modules. Up to quasi-equivalence, this defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor

\displaystyle\Psi_{\downarrow}:\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G),

which sends a
G
-invariant exact cylindrical Lagrangian
L
to its reduction
\underline{L}
.

An similar argument also applies to
\mathcal{B}_{G}(-,\underline{L})
, yielding a corresponding
A_{\infty}
-functor

\displaystyle\Psi_{\uparrow}:\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)_{0},

which sends a reduced Lagrangian
\underline{L}
to its preimage
L\subset\mu^{-1}(0)
. Hence,
\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)
is quasi-equivalent to this full subcategory of
\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)
.

Convention 6.1
.

To simplify notation, from now on we will drop the subscript
0
and, whenever we refer to the equivariant Fukaya category, implicitly restrict to the full subcategory consisting of Lagrangians contained in the zero level set of the moment map.

The construction was first introduced in
[
45
]
and, in the exact setting, further developed by
[
21
]
. See also
[
33
,
20
]
for related constructions. We adapt the classical construction to the Borel construction.

6.1.
Moment correspondence

Definition 6.2
.

Let
(X,\lambda_{X}),(Y,\lambda_{Y})
be Liouville manifolds. An
exact Lagrangian correspondence
from
X
to
Y
is an exact Lagrangian submanifold

L_{X,Y}\subset(X^{-}\times Y,-\mathrm{pr}_{X}^{*}\lambda_{X}+\mathrm{pr}_{Y}^{*}\lambda_{Y}),

where
X^{-}
denotes
X
equipped with the negated Liouville form
-\lambda_{X}
.

In the setting of Liouville reduction, the moment correspondence provides a canonical choice for Lagrangian correspondence.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
X
be a Liouville manifold equipped with a Liouville action of a Lie group
G
. The
moment correspondence
is defined as the Lagrangian submanifold

\displaystyle L^{\pi}\coloneqq\left\{(x,[x])\in X^{-}\times X/\!\!/G\mid x\in\mu^{-1}(0)\right\}\subset X^{-}\times X/\!\!/G.

The fact that
L^{\pi}
is Lagrangian follows directly from the defining property of symplectic reduction.
Moreover, since the
G
-action preserves the Liouville structure, we obtain the following characterization.

Lemma 6.4
.

The moment correspondence
L^{\pi}
is an exact, cylindrical Lagrangian submanifold.

Proof.

Since
\lambda_{X/\!\!/G}
is obtained by descending the
G
-invariant Liouville form
\lambda_{X}|_{\mu_{X}^{-1}(0)}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{X^{-}\times(X/\!\!/G)}|_{L^{\pi}}=0.

Hence
L^{\pi}
is an exact Lagrangian submanifold with constant primitive. In particular, this implies that the Liouville vector field is tangent to
L^{\pi}
everywhere, since
Zf=0
for any function
f
vanishing on
L^{\pi}
.
∎

Definition 6.5
.

A tuple of exact cylindrical
G
-invariant Lagrangians

(L_{0},L_{1},\cdots,L_{d})\subset\mu^{-1}(0)

is said to be
G
-composable
if it satisfies the following equivalent conditions:

(1)

For any
i,j
, the intersection
L_{i}\cap L_{j}
is clean and consists of a (possibly empty) union of
G
-orbits of single points; that is,

(6.2)

\displaystyle L_{i}\cap L_{j}=\bigsqcup_{\alpha}G\cdot z_{\alpha}.

(2)

The corresponding reduced Lagrangians
\underline{L}_{i}\subset X\sslash G
intersect pairwise transversely.

(3)

The product
L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}\subset X^{-}\times(X/\!\!/G)
intersects the moment correspondence
L^{\pi}
cleanly along the
G
-orbits of the form
(G\cdot z_{\alpha})\times z_{\alpha}
, where
z_{\alpha}
denotes both the intersection point of
\underline{L}_{i}\cap\underline{L}_{j}
and a chosen representative of its lift in
\mu^{-1}(0)
.

For a general tuple of Lagrangians, we may replace each Lagrangian by an isomorphic object so that the resulting tuple becomes
G
-composable. In Condition (2), by requirement of pairwise transversality, we also exclude triple or higher intersections. For a pair of Lagrangians
(L_{i},\underline{L}_{j})\subset X^{-}\times(X/\!\!/G)
, we similarly say it is
G
-composable if the pair
(L_{i},L_{j})
is
G
-composable.

Formally, we will consider Lagrangian correspondence

L_{G}^{\pi}\subset X^{-}_{G}\times X/\!\!/G,

obtained as the direct limit of a sequence of finite-dimensional approximations, which fits into the diagram

(6.3)

\hbox to271.03pt{\vbox to53.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 135.5157pt\lower-26.84726pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Correspondingly, for each pair of
G
-composable Lagrangians
L_{i},L_{j}\subset\mu^{-1}(0)
, we consider Lagrangian Borel spaces
(L_{i}(N)\times\underline{L}_{j})
in
X^{-}(N)\times X/\!\!/G
lying over the zero section of
T^{*}BG(N)
.

Before proceeding with the detailed construction, let us briefly outline the main ideas underlying the bilinear functor (
6.1
) and its representable property.

It is a classical fact that for a pair of Lagrangians
L
and
K
intersecting cleanly, there exists a spectral sequence converging to the Floer cohomology
HF^{\bullet}(L,K)
; see, for example,
[
57
,
62
,
59
]
. The
E_{1}
-page of this spectral sequence takes the form

(6.4)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{p,q}=\begin{cases}HF^{p+q,\mathrm{loc}}(L,K;C_{p})&$1\leq p\leq r$\\
0&otherwise\end{cases}.

Here
C_{1},\cdots,C_{r}
are the connected components of
L\cap K
, ordered according to the values of the action on the constant paths. The
local Floer cohomology group

HF^{p+q,\mathrm{loc}}
is defined by counting pseudo-holomorphic strips whose images are contained in a neighborhood of connected component
C_{l}
. The filtration underlying this spectral sequence is defined by

(6.5)

\displaystyle F^{p}CF^{\bullet}(L,K)\coloneqq\{x\in CF^{\bullet}(L,K)\mid x\not\in C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{r}\}.

Since the Floer differential increases the action, it preserves this filtration.

Each local Floer cohomology group
HF^{\mathrm{loc}}(L,K;C_{l})
can be identified with the singular cohomology
H^{\bullet}(C_{l})
, up to a grading shift determined by Robbin-Salamon index
\mu(C_{l})
. In the special case where the intersection
L\cap K
is transverse, the local Floer group reduces to
HF^{\mathrm{loc}}(L,K;C_{l})=\mathbb{K}
.

In our case, the clean intersection between
L^{\pi}
and
L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}
are given by
G
-orbits, and hence are invariant under the
G
-action. This invariance allows us to identify the connected component
C_{l}
of
L^{\pi}\cap\left(L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}\right)
across the fibers of the Borel construction (
6.3
); for simplicity, we continue to denote them by
C_{l}
.

The above identifications also extend to the primitives and hence to the values of the action functional. For future reference, we formalize this as follows.

Remark 6.6

(Continuation of Section
3.2
)
.

By Lemma
6.4
, the primitive of
L^{\pi}
may be chosen to be zero. Thus the value of the action functional (defined using
-\lambda_{X}+\lambda_{X/\!\!/G}
) on
L^{\pi}\cap\left(L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}\right)
is simply
f_{L_{j}}-f_{L_{i}}
(note that
L_{i}\subset X^{-}
contributes the opposite primitive).

In other words, under the natural identifications among:

(1)

the connected components of clean intersection
L_{i}\cap L_{j}
,

(2)

the transverse intersection points
\underline{L}_{i}\cap\underline{L}_{j}
,

(3)

the connected components of
L^{\pi}\cap\left(L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}\right)
,

(4)

the corresponding components in the Borel–Liouville construction,

the respective action functionals take the same value
f_{L_{j}}-f_{L_{i}}
.

If the components are ordered so that

\displaystyle f_{L_{j}}-f_{L_{i}}|_{C_{1}}<\cdots<f_{L_{j}}-f_{L_{i}}|_{C_{r}},

then this ordering is preserved under all of the identifications above.

The above discussion is carried out under the assumption
H=0
. In general case, we may restrict
H
to a sufficiently small neighborhood of
0
in the space of admissible Hamiltonians, ensuring that its contribution to the action functional is small enough that will not alter the ordering. A more convenient approach, avoiding the Hamiltonian, is to work entirely in the Morse–Bott model, see Remark
6.7
below.

We may define the local Floer cohomology group in Borel construction by considering the Floer strips (
4.7
) whose Hamiltonian chords and images lie in a small neighborhood of
C_{l}
.

Taking the inverse limit with respect to the approximations yields a spectral sequence whose
E_{1}
-page is given by

(6.6)

\displaystyle E_{1}^{p,q}=\begin{cases}HF^{p+q,\mathrm{loc}}_{G}(L^{\pi},L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j};C_{p})&$1\leq p\leq r$\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

As shown in Section
4.6
, there is an isomorphism
HF^{\mathrm{loc}}_{G}(L^{\pi},L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j};C_{l})\cong H_{G}^{\bullet}(C_{l})
up to a grading shift (cf. Remark
6.7
).

Since the
G
-action is free, we have

(6.7)

\displaystyle H_{G}^{\bullet}(C_{l})\cong H^{\bullet}(C_{l}/G)\cong HF^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{L}_{i},\underline{L}_{j}),

since
C_{l}/G
is precisely the transverse intersection between the reduced Lagrangians
\underline{L}_{i}
and
\underline{L}_{j}
. On the other hand, by the identification between
L^{\pi}\cap\left(L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j}\right)
and
L_{i}\cap L_{j}
described in Definition
6.5
, we obtain

(6.8)

HF^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}_{G}(L^{\pi},L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j};C_{l})\cong H_{G}^{\bullet}(C_{l})\cong HF_{G}^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}(L_{i},L_{j};C_{l}).

As a result, we obtain a naive identification between the
E_{1}
-pages of two spectral sequence converging to
HF^{\bullet}(\underline{L}_{i},\underline{L}_{j})
and
HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L_{i},L_{j})
respectively. The remaining task is to strengthen this identification to an isomorphism between spectral sequences, and moreover, to promote it to a representable
A_{\infty}
-functor at the categorical level.

Remark 6.7

(Morse-Bott model)
.

Since the relevant intersections occur cleanly in our case, it is convenient to adapt the Definition
5.7
to the “Morse-Bott” model; see, for example,
[
10
,
59
]
.

For a cleanly intersecting pair of Lagrangians
(L,K)
, we choose a Morse function on each connected component of
L\cap K
and work with hybrid configurations consisting of a pseudo-holomorphic disk together with Morse trajectories connecting boundary marked points of the disk to critical points of the chosen Morse functions. Equivalently, one may require that each boundary marked point lies on the stable or unstable manifolds of a Morse critical point.

This provides a realization of the spectral sequence (
6.4
) obtained by filtering the Floer complex by energy. The
E_{1}
-page is computed by the local Morse cohomology on the clean intersection components (see, for example,
[
57
,
59
]
).

Returning to the Borel construction, we choose a Morse function for each connected component

C_{l}\hookrightarrow L^{\pi}\cap(L_{i}\times\underline{L}_{j})\hookrightarrow L^{\pi}_{G}\cap\left(L_{i,G}\times\underline{L}_{j}\right)\rightarrow 0_{BG}

over critical points of Morse function on
0_{BG}
. The hybrid objects we count are pseudo-holomorphic strips parametrized by Morse trajectories (Definition
4.7
with no Hamiltonians) whose boundary marked point are connected to critical points in these components by (families of) Morse trajectories (
4.32
). This is analogous to the PSS-type configurations considered earlier in Section
4.6
.

Then the filtration by energy produces the spectral sequence (
6.6
), and the
E_{1}
-page is computed by (families of) Morse trajectories in these components. The argument in Section
4.6
shows that its
E_{1}
-page is isomorphic to
G
-equivariant cohomology of each connected component
C_{l}
(up to a shift of grading).

6.2.
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

To define the bimodule

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}:\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)\rightarrow\mathbf{Ch},

we proceed in a manner analogous to Definition
5.7
. We consider objects consisting of a Morse tree together with a fiberwise pseudo-holomorphic strip mapping to the Borel space (
6.3
).

For the Morse tree part, we now require the existence of one distinguished incoming semi-infinite edge and one outgoing edge.

Remark 6.8
.

From this point, we will no longer refer explicitly to the finite-dimensional approximation construction.
All pseudo-holomorphic strips or disks mapping to
X_{G}
, together with their boundary and asymptotic conditions, are understood as in Definition
4.7
and Definition
5.7
.

For example, a pseudo-holomorphic strip
u:Z\rightarrow L_{G}
should always be interpreted as a pair
(\gamma,u)
exactly as in Definition
4.7
; the same convention applies to disks, and all associated asymptotic and boundary conditions.

The quilted strip is illustrated in Figure
3
. It can be viewed either as a pair of pseudo-holomorphic strips mapping to
X/\!\!/G
and
X_{G}
respectively, or equivalently as a single pseudo-holomorphic map to
X_{G}^{-}\times X/\!\!/G
via the standard “folding strip” argument. See for example
[
45
]
.

Figure 3.
Quilted Strips Defining the Bimodule.

The quilted maps
u=(u_{1},u_{2}):\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}\rightarrow X/\!\!/G\times X_{G}
with Lagrangian boundary conditions
(\underline{L}^{\prime}_{0},\cdots\underline{L}^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})\subseteq X/\!\!/G
,
(L_{0,G},\cdots,L_{k,G})\subseteq X_{G}
and seam condition
L^{\pi}_{G}
, are described as follows.

•

The domain
\Sigma\coloneqq[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}

is a bordered quilted strip with two patches
\Sigma_{1}\coloneqq[-1,0]\times\mathbb{R}
and
\Sigma_{2}\coloneqq[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
with a seam
\sigma\coloneqq\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
. The left and right boundary components are denoted by
\sigma^{(1)}=\{-1\}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma^{(2)}=\{1\}\times\mathbb{R}
.

•

Let
z^{(1)}=(z^{(1)}_{1},\cdots z^{(1)}_{k^{\prime}})
(resp.
z^{(2)}=(z^{(2)}_{1},\cdots z^{(2)}_{k})
) be boundary marked points on
\sigma^{(1)}
(resp.
\sigma^{(2)}
) such that for any
j_{1}<j_{2}
, we have
\operatorname{Im}z^{(i)}_{j_{1}}>\operatorname{Im}z^{(i)}_{j_{2}}
.

•

For
j=0,\dots k
(resp.
j^{\prime}=0,\dots k^{\prime}
), we denote the boundary segments between marked points by

\sigma^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}}\coloneqq\{-1\}\times[\operatorname{Im}z^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}+1},\operatorname{Im}z^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}}],\quad\sigma^{(2)}_{j}\coloneqq\{1\}\times[\operatorname{Im}z^{(2)}_{j+1},\operatorname{Im}z^{(2)}_{j}],

with the convention
\operatorname{Im}z^{(i)}_{0}\coloneqq+\infty
and
\operatorname{Im}z^{(1)}_{k^{\prime}+1}\coloneqq-\infty\eqqcolon\operatorname{Im}z^{(2)}_{k+1}
. Note that

\sigma^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}-1}\cap\sigma^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}}=\{z^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}}\},\quad\sigma^{(2)}_{j-1}\cap\sigma^{(2)}_{j}=\{z^{(2)}_{j}\}.

•

A quilted map consists of

\displaystyle u_{1}:\Sigma_{1}\rightarrow(X/\!\!/G,J_{X/\!\!/G}),\quad u_{2}:\Sigma_{2}\rightarrow(X_{G},J_{X_{G}}),

each pseudo-holomorphic with respect to the corresponding almost complex structures, subject to:

–

[Boundary conditions] For each boundary segment,

u_{1}|_{\sigma^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}}}:\sigma^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow\underline{L}^{{}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\subset X/\!\!/G,\quad u_{2}|_{\sigma^{(2)}_{j}}:\sigma^{(2)}_{j}\rightarrow L_{j,G}\subset X_{G}.

In particular, at the marked points:
u_{1}(z^{(1)}_{j^{\prime}})\in\underline{L}^{{}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}-1}\cap\underline{L}^{{}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}
and
u_{2}(z^{(2)}_{j})\in L_{j-1,G}\cap L_{j,G}
.

–

[Seam condition] Along the seam:
(u_{1},u_{2})|_{\sigma}:\sigma\rightarrow L^{\pi}_{G}
.

–

[Asymptotic conditions] For any
t_{1}\in[-1,0]
(resp.
t_{2}\in[0,1]
), the following limits

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\pm\infty}u_{1}(t_{1},\tau)\eqqcolon q_{\pm\infty}\in X/\!\!/G,\quad\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\pm\infty}u_{2}(t_{2},\tau)\eqqcolon p_{\pm\infty}\in X_{G}

exist and are independent of
t_{i}
.
The seam conditions forces

\displaystyle(q_{+\infty},p_{+\infty})

\displaystyle\in L^{\pi}_{G}\cap(\underline{L}^{{}^{\prime}}_{0}\times L_{0,G})

\displaystyle(q_{-\infty},p_{-\infty})

\displaystyle\in L^{\pi}_{G}\cap(\underline{L}^{{}^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\times L_{k,G}).

These limiting points correspond either to generators of the equivariant Floer complexes or connecting to critical points of Morse functions, depending on whether we work in the Floer or Morse–Bott model (cf. Remark
6.7
).

–

[Stability conditions]
The automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}(u)
is finite.

Suppose all tuples are
G
-composable in the sense of Definition
6.5
. After choosing the perturbation data generically, transversality is achieved. Assume further that the sequences satisfy
L_{0}>\cdots>L_{k-1}>L_{k}
and
\underline{L}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}>\cdots\underline{L}_{1}^{\prime}>\underline{L}_{0}^{\prime}
so that the maximum principal applies. We apply the Gromov–Floer compactification and count the zero-dimensional moduli spaces, this defines the bimodule structure. An argument analogous to that in Lemma
4.12
shows that these bimodule strucutres commute with the projection
P_{N}
, and we can take the inverse limit to obtain the following bimodule strucutre.

\displaystyle\mu^{k|1|k^{\prime}}:CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{k},L_{k-1})\otimes\cdots\otimes CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{1},L_{0})\otimes CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{0},L^{\pi},\underline{L}^{\prime}_{0})\otimes CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L}_{0}^{\prime},\underline{L}_{1}^{\prime})\otimes\cdots

\displaystyle\otimes CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L}_{k^{\prime}-1}^{\prime},\underline{L}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\rightarrow CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L_{k},L^{\pi},\underline{L}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

As before, we can choose the Floer data coherently across all moduli spaces, which produces an
(\mathcal{O}_{G},\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G})
-bimodule

(6.10)

\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq\left\{CF_{G}^{\bullet}(-,L^{\pi},-),\left\{\mu^{k|1|k^{\prime}}\right\}_{k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\right\}.

Localizing with respect to quasi-units
\mathcal{Z}_{G}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X/\!\!/G}
defines a
(\mathcal{W}_{G}(X),\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G))
-bimodule, which we denote by

(6.11)

\mathcal{B}_{G}\coloneqq{}_{\mathcal{Z}_{G}^{-1}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}.

We remark that because the wrapping Hamiltonian
H
is
G
-invariant, its flow induces wrapping both in
X
and in
X/\!\!/G
. Thus the wrapping sequences in
\mathcal{O}_{G}
and
\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G}
agree under the natural identification of
L_{i}^{k}\Leftrightarrow\underline{L}_{i}^{k}
.

Definition 6.9
.

(
[
18
]
)

Let
L
be a
G
-invariant Lagrangian. We say an element
\mathbf{1}_{L}\in CF^{0}_{G}(L,L^{\pi},\underline{L})
is:

(1)

left cyclic
if it is
\mu^{0|1|0}
-closed and

\mu^{1|1|0}(-,\mathbf{1}_{L}):CF_{G}^{\bullet}(K,L)\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}_{G}(K,L^{\pi},\underline{L})

is a quasi-isomorphism for all
K
.

(2)

right cyclic
if it is
\mu^{0|1|0}
-closed and

\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)\colon CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L},\underline{K})\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L^{\pi},\underline{K})

is a quasi-isomorphism for all
\underline{K}
.

(3)

bicyclic
if it is both left cyclic and right cyclic.

Remark 6.10
.

From the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule equations satisfied by the operations
\{\mu^{k|1|k^{\prime}}\}
, it follows that the collection of maps

\displaystyle\{\mu^{k|1|0}(-,\mathbf{a})\}

defines a morphism of left
A_{\infty}
-modules

\displaystyle CF^{\bullet}_{G}(-,L)\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}_{G}(-,L^{\pi},\underline{L})

whenever
\mathbf{a}
is
\mu^{0|1|0}
-closed.
In particular, having a
left cyclic element

\mathbf{1}_{L}
is equivalent to requiring that

\displaystyle\{\mu^{k|1|0}(-,\mathbf{1}_{L})\}:CF^{\bullet}_{G}(-,L)\longrightarrow CF^{\bullet}_{G}(-,L^{\pi},\underline{L})

is a quasi-isomorphism of left
\mathcal{O}_{G}
-modules.
Analogous characterizations for right cyclic and bicyclic elements are formulated in the same way.

By the connectedness assumption on
L
,
L^{\pi}
intersects
L\times\underline{L}
cleanly along a single connected component
C_{0}
, namely
L
itself. Hence the
E_{1}
-page of the spectral sequence (
6.6
) has only one nonzero row
E_{1}^{0,q}
. Therefore, it degenerates at the
E_{1}
-page, yielding an isomorphism

(6.12)

\displaystyle H_{G}^{\bullet}(L)\cong HF_{G}^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}(L^{\pi},L\times\underline{L};C_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:G_spectral_sequence}}}{{\cong}}HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L^{\pi},\underline{L}).

The first map is defined by a PSS-type map supported near
C_{0}
, in complete analogy with the equivariant PSS map from Section
4.6
(cf. Remark
6.7
).
We denote the cochain-level map of (
6.12
) by

\displaystyle\Psi_{L}\colon C_{G}^{\bullet}(L)\rightarrow CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L^{\pi},\,L\times\underline{L};\,C_{0}).

Definition 6.11
.

Define

\mathbf{1}_{L}\coloneqq\Psi_{L}(c_{\min},x_{\min})\in CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L^{\pi},(L\times\underline{L}))

where
c_{\min}
is the sum of minima of the Morse functions chosen on
0_{BG}
and
x_{\min}
is the sum of minima of the Morse function on
L
, viewed as the connected component of
L^{\pi}\cap(L\times\underline{L})
.

In other words,
\mathbf{1}_{L}
is the cochain level representative of the unit transferred via (
6.12
). Also notice that by Condition (5) of Definition
3.7
and the assumption that the Morse function on
L
is linear near infinity, there are only finitely many critical points contributing to these sums. Therefore,
\mathbf{1}_{L}
is well-defined.

Lemma 6.12
.

The element
\mathbf{1}_{L}
is bicyclic.

Proof.

By construction,
\mathbf{1}_{L}
is closed. To establish right cyclicity, it suffices to show that

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-):CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L},\underline{K})\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L^{\pi},\underline{K}).

induces isomorphisms between cohomologies.

Because the action functionals on both sides agree under the identifications of Remark
6.6
, the map (
6.13
) preserves the energy filtrations. Therefore, it induces a morphism of the corresponding spectral sequences. On the
E_{1}
-page, it is given by

[\mu^{0|1|1,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)_{C_{l}}]:HF^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{L},\underline{K};C_{l}/G)\to HF^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}_{G}(L^{\pi},L\times\underline{K};C_{l}),

where
C_{l}
denotes a connected component of the clean intersection, and
\mu^{0|1|1,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)_{C_{l}}
is defined by the same moduli spaces as in (
6.13
), except localized to a sufficiently small neighborhood of
C_{l}
.

Recall that, by (
6.7
), both sides are identified with
H_{G}^{\bullet}(C_{l})\cong H^{\bullet}(C_{l}/G)
as vector spaces. Therefore, it suffices to show that
[\mu^{0|1|1,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)_{C_{l}}]
is
injective
.

To this end, we define a map
F:CF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L^{\pi},\underline{K})\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L},\underline{K})
by reversing the roles of the input and output of
\mu^{0|1|1}(-,-)
and we show that
F
provides a left inverse to
[\mu^{0|1|1,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)_{C_{l}}]
; see Figure
4
.

Figure 4.
Composition of
[\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)]
and its left inverse.

As depicted by Figure
4
, the composition

F\circ\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)

is homotopic to

\mu_{\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G}}^{2}(F(\mathbf{1}_{L}),-).

Passing to the spectral sequence, we obtain

(6.14)

[F^{\mathrm{loc}}]\circ[\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)]\sim[\mu_{\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G}}^{2,\mathrm{loc}}]\left([F(\mathbf{1}_{L})],-\right),

where
F^{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G}}^{2,\mathrm{loc}}
are defined by restricting
F
and
\mu_{\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G}}^{2}
to those contributions whose outputs lie in the component
C_{l}
.

We claim that

(6.15)

[F(\mathbf{1}_{L})]=e_{\underline{L}}\in HF^{\bullet}(\underline{L},\underline{L}).

Indeed, when both input and output are the element
\mathbf{1}_{L}
, the only quilted configurations that can contribute to the strip component in Figure
4
are constant quilts.
Therefore, the relevant moduli space is identified with constant Morse trajectories at the minimum
(c_{\min},x_{\min})
, which implies that
F(\mathbf{1}_{L})
represents the unit class.

Combining (
6.14
) with (
6.15
), we conclude that

[F^{\mathrm{loc}}]\circ[\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)]=\mathrm{Id}_{HF^{\bullet,\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{L},\underline{K},C_{l}/G)}.

Thus
[\mu^{0|1|1,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)]
is injective. This completes the proof of the right cyclic property of
\mathbf{1}_{L}
.

A similar argument applied to

(6.16)

\displaystyle\mu^{1|1|0}(-,\mathbf{1}_{L}):CF_{G}^{\bullet}(K,L)\rightarrow CF^{\bullet}_{G}(K,L^{\pi},\underline{L})

shows that it is also a quasi-isomorphism. The proof is now complete.
∎

Corollary 6.13
.

The
A_{\infty}
-functor defined by the bimodule
\mathcal{Q}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\colon\mathcal{O}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{X\sslash G}},\qquad L\longmapsto\mathcal{Q}(L,-),

lands in representable right
\mathcal{O}_{X\sslash G}
-modules, each of which is represented by the reduced Lagrangian
\underline{L}
.

Similarly, the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X\sslash G}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{\mathcal{O}_{G}},\qquad\underline{K}\longmapsto\mathcal{Q}(-,\underline{K}),

lands in representable left
\mathcal{O}_{G}
-modules, represented by the G-invariant Lagrangian
K
.

Proof.

By the bicyclicity of the element
\mathbf{1}_{L}
, we obtain quasi-isomorphisms

(6.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(-,\underline{K})

\displaystyle=CF^{\bullet}_{G}(-,L^{\pi},\underline{K})\xleftarrow[\sim]{\left\{\mu^{k|1|0}(-,\mathbf{1}_{K})\right\}}CF_{G}^{\bullet}(-,K),

(6.18)

\displaystyle CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L},-)\xrightarrow[\sim]{\left\{\mu^{0|1|k}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)\right\}}\mathcal{Q}(L,-)

\displaystyle=CF^{\bullet}_{G}(L,L^{\pi},-).

between left
\mathcal{O}_{G}
-modules and right
\mathcal{O}_{X\sslash G}
-modules, respectively; see Remark
6.10
.
Here “quasi-isomorphism” means a pointwise quasi-isomorphism, i.e. for each test object the maps

\mu^{1|1|0}(-,\mathbf{1}_{K})
and
\mu^{0|1|1}(\mathbf{1}_{L},-)
induce quasi-isomorphisms of cochain complexes.

Thus the cohomology functor
H^{\bullet}\mathcal{Q}(-,\underline{K})

is pointwise isomorphic to the left
H^{\bullet}\mathcal{O}_{G}
-module obtained via the Yoneda embedding

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}\mathcal{O}_{G}\hookrightarrow H^{\bullet}\mathrm{Mod}^{\mathcal{O}_{G}},\quad K\longmapsto H\mathcal{O}^{\bullet}_{G}(-,K).

Since we work over a field (so all
\mathcal{O}_{G}
-modules are cofibrant), this cohomology representability is sufficient to conclude that the
A_{\infty}
-module
\mathcal{Q}(-,\underline{K})
is representable, This follows from an
A_{\infty}
-version of Whitehead’s theorem; see
[
20
, Appendix A]
. An identical argument applies to the other functor, showing that it is likewise representable.
∎

In particular, combining (
6.17
) with (
6.18
), we obtain a quasi-isomorphism of cochain complexes

(6.19)

\displaystyle CF^{\bullet}(\underline{L},\underline{K})\;\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\;CF_{G}^{\bullet}(L,K).

A similar argument to that of Proposition
5.14
and Corollary
5.15
gives rise to the following corollary.

Corollary 6.14
.

The following diagram

(6.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(L^{k+1},\underline{K}^{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(L^{k+1},\underline{K}^{j+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(L^{k},\underline{K}^{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(L^{k},\underline{K}^{j+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{G}_{L_{0}^{k},L_{1}^{j}}}

commutes up to homotopy.
Here, the horizontal and vertical arrows are given by multiplication by the images of the tensor products of units and quasi-units under the Künneth isomorphism

HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L^{\prime}\times\underline{K}^{\prime},L\times\underline{K})\cong HF^{\bullet}_{G}(L^{\prime},L)\otimes HF^{\bullet}(\underline{K}^{\prime},\underline{K}).

For example, the upper horizontal arrow is given by multiplication with
\mathrm{id}_{L^{k+1}}\otimes e_{\underline{K}^{j}}
where
\mathrm{id}_{L^{k+1}}
denote the unit in
HF_{G}^{0}(L^{k+1},L^{k+1})
, and
e_{\underline{K}^{j}}
denotes the quasi-unit in
HF^{0}(\underline{K}^{j},\underline{K}^{j+1})
.

In particular, this implies the left locality of the direct limit, i.e.

(6.21)

\varinjlim_{j}H\mathcal{Q}^{\bullet}(L^{k+1},\underline{K}^{j})\cong\varinjlim_{j}H\mathcal{Q}^{\bullet}(L^{k},\underline{K}^{j}).

Once the isomorphism (
6.19
) and its functoriality have been established, passing to the localized category (wrapped Fukaya cateogry) is immediate and entirely formal. We follow the localization framework of
[
20
]
, adapting their construction to the present equivariant setting.

Theorem 6.15
.

The
A_{\infty}
-functor associated to the bimodule
\mathcal{B}_{G}
defined in (
6.11
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{G}:\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)},\quad L\mapsto\mathcal{B}_{G}(L,-)\coloneqq{}_{\mathcal{Z}_{G}^{-1}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}(L,-)

lands in representable right modules. Thus, it induces an
A_{\infty}
-functor

\displaystyle\Phi_{\downarrow}:\mathcal{W}_{G}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G),

well-defined up to quasi-equivalence.

Proof.

A module analogue of the argument in Corollary
5.17
(see
[
19
, Corollary 3.38]
) implies that the cohomology of the right localization
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}
is computed by taking colimit over the wrapping sequence in
\mathcal{O}_{X/\!\!/G}
. Combined with (
6.18
), we obtain

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}(L,-)\simeq HW^{\bullet}(\underline{L},-)\in\mathrm{Mod}_{H^{\bullet}\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)}

In particular,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}
is
\mathcal{Z}_{G}
-local on the left, and therefore the map
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}(L,-)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{G}(L,-)
is a quasi-isomorphism by
[
19
, Lemma 3.13]
.

Finally, since
CF_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}^{\bullet}(\underline{L},-)
is quasi-isomorphic to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X/\!\!/G}}(L,-)
and is, by definition, the Yoneda module represented by
\underline{L}
, we conclude that
\mathcal{B}_{G}(L,-)
is represented by
\underline{L}
by
[
20
, Appendix.A3]
. This completes the proof.
∎

A similar argument applied to
\underline{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{G}(-,\underline{L})
produces the functor in the opposite direction, namely an
A_{\infty}
-functor

\displaystyle\Psi_{\uparrow}:\mathcal{W}(X/\!\!/G)\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{G}(X),

which sends a reduced Lagrangian
\underline{L}
to its preimage
L
.

7.
Symplectic quotient with singularities

In the previous section, we assumed that the Hamiltonian group action on the zero level set of the moment map is free. Unfortunately, this assumption fails in many interesting examples, leading to a singular exact symplectic quotient.
In this section, we focus on the simplest type of such singularities, arising from the quotient of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by the Hamiltonian
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action with weights
(1,-1)
.

7.1.
SNC divisor complement

We briefly recall a construction from
[
49
]
of a Liouville structure on the complement of a divisor. Let

K=Z(s)\subset Y

be a simple normal crossing divisor in a smooth projective variety
Y
, determined by a very ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}
. Here
s
denotes a defining section and we write the smooth irreducible components of
K
as
K_{i}
. We denote the complement of the divisor by
X\coloneqq Y\setminus K
. Note that
X
is a complex affine variety, and any complex affine variety can be presented in this way.

Fix a Hermitian metric
\lVert\cdot\rVert
on
\mathcal{L}
, and define

(7.1)

\omega\coloneqq-dd^{c}\log\lVert s\rVert^{2},\quad\lambda\coloneqq-d^{c}\log\lVert s\rVert^{2}.

Choose a regular open neighborhood
U\supset K
that is diffeomorphic to (a smoothing of corners of) the union
\bigcup_{i}N^{\leq\epsilon}_{K_{i}/Y}
of disk bundles. The positivity of
K
ensures that
\lambda
defines a Liouville form on
Y\setminus U
with respect to the symplectic form
\omega
. By abuse of notation, we denote this Liouville domain by
X
, and denote its completion by
\hat{X}
.

This particular Liouville form
\lambda
has simple Reeb dynamics at
\partial X
. There is a projection

p:\partial X\to K

whose restriction to
K_{i}^{\circ}\coloneqq K_{i}-\bigcup_{j\neq i}K_{j}
is a principal
S^{1}
-bundle by
[
49
, Section 5]
. Its angular form is given by

\lambda|_{p^{-1}(K_{i}^{\circ})}=-\Theta,\quad\Theta\coloneqq\text{Hermitian connection 1-form of $\|\cdot\|$}.

This means that the induced
S^{1}
-action is generated by the Reeb flow of the minimal period
2\pi
. This is a consequence of the simplicity of the divisor
K
.

Remark 7.1
.

The
S^{1}
-action generated by the Reeb flow is distinct from the given Hamiltonian
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action, hence we use a different notation. Note that the above construction extends to the equivariant setting, provided that the divisor
K
is invariant under the group action under consideration.

7.2.
Conic fibration

Let
\underline{X}
be a smooth quasi-projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
equipped with a positive line bundle
\mathcal{L}\to\underline{X}
. Let
\underline{D}
be a smooth ample divisor defined by a section
s\in\Gamma(\underline{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2}).
The
conic fibration
associated with this data is the following variety with a prescribed
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action, which canonically extends to Kähler
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action:

\mathbb{S}^{1}\circlearrowright X\coloneqq Z(zw=s)\subset\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{L}^{\oplus 2}),\quad c\cdot(\mathbf{x},z,w)=(\mathbf{x},cz,c^{-1}w),

where
(z,w)
denotes the fiber coordinates. The pullback divisor
D\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(\underline{D})
is a normal crossings divisor with two irreducible components,

D=D_{1}+D_{2},\quad D_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Tot}_{\underline{D}}(\mathcal{L}\oplus 0),\quad D_{2}\coloneqq\mathrm{Tot}_{\underline{D}}(0\oplus\mathcal{L}).

In the language of geometric invariant theory,
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbits of
X
are of the following types:

•

the stable locus
X^{s}\coloneqq X\setminus D
;

•

the strictly semistable locus
X^{s.s}\coloneqq D
; and

•

the polystable locus
X^{p.s}\coloneqq D_{1}\cap D_{2}=\mathrm{Tot}_{\underline{D}}(0\oplus 0)
.

We introduce some notations that will be used throughout the rest of the section. We denote the polystable locus by
F
, emphasizing that it coincides with the fixed-point locus of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action. Let
N
be a regular neighborhood of
F
, symplectomorphic to the disk bundle
DN_{F/X}
. Locally near the
F
,
X
is modeled on a product with the standard Lefschetz fibration
zw:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
. If
V
is a small trivializing neighborhood of
\mathcal{L}|_{\underline{D}}
, then we have

(7.2)

\hbox to249.51pt{\vbox to55.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 124.75298pt\lower-27.57558pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.55 -24.12 L 84.04 -24.12}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{19.91362pt}{-17.43254pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.71 C 0.95 2.71 1.72 2.1 1.72 1.36 C 1.72 0.61 0.95 0 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{60.93503pt}{-17.43254pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We denote the regular locus of
X
and its quotient, respectively, by

X^{\circ}\coloneqq X^{s},\quad\underline{X}^{\circ}\coloneqq\underline{X}\setminus\underline{D}.

From now on, we impose the following assumptions on
\underline{X}
:

•

there exists a smooth projective compactification
\underline{Y}\supset\underline{X}
with a SNC boundary divisor
\underline{K}
;

•

the space
\underline{X}
is equipped with a Liouville structure described in (
7.1
);

•

the divisor
\underline{D}
extends to a smooth ample divisor on
\underline{Y}
. Abusing the notation, we denote the extension by
\underline{D}
.

Under these assumptions, we can realize
X
as a divisor complement as follows. Define

Y\coloneqq Z(zw=su^{2})\subset\mathbb{P}_{\underline{Y}}(\mathcal{L}^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O})\xrightarrow{\pi}\underline{Y},

equipped with the natural extension of the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action. It is straightforward to check that
X=Y\setminus(K+H_{\infty})
where
K\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(\underline{K})
and
H_{\infty}
denotes the hyperplane divisor at infinity. Note that both divisors are
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-invariant.

Next, we describe an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-equivariant Liouville structure on

X^{\circ}=Y\setminus(K+D+H_{\infty})=X\setminus D.

The construction is motivated by Kirwan’s desingularization process
[
34
]
. We first perform an equivariant blow-up along the polystable locus,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{1}\circlearrowright\mathrm{Bl}_{F}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

equipped with the lifted moment map
\tilde{\mu}
. In the original desingularization process, one removes the strict transform
\widetilde{X}^{s.s}
of the strictly semistable locus
X^{s.s}
. In our setting, we further excise a neighborhood of the exceptional divisor. More precisely, we define

X^{\circ}\cong\mathrm{Bl}_{F}X\setminus(D+E).

This is symplectomorphic to the original regular locus
X^{\circ}=X^{s}
by
[
34
]
. Since
K+D+E+H_{\infty}
is very ample and simple normal crossing, we equip
X^{\circ}
with the Liouville form defined in (
7.1
), and denote the resulting Liouville manifold by

(X^{\circ},\lambda^{\circ}).

Because the divisor is
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-equivariant and the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action is free on its complement,
\lambda^{\circ}
descends to a Liouville form
(\underline{X}^{\circ},\underline{\lambda}^{\circ})
by Theorem
2.10
.

Since
X
is already realized as a complement of divisors, it is worth explaining the role of the blow-up. Locally, the blow-up diagram above is modeled on

(7.3)

V\times\left(\hbox to206.71pt{\vbox to54.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 103.35489pt\lower-27.10728pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

We focus on the symplectic and Liouville structures on the right-hand side of the diagram. The form
\widetilde{\omega}
agrees with the pullback of
\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
away from the blow-up locus. On the other hand, the Liouville form is modified along the Liouville isotopy
i:(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2\circ}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
.

The advantages of the Liouville form
\lambda^{\circ}
over
\lambda_{\mathrm{std}}
are as follows. First,
\lambda^{\circ}
simplifies the Reeb dynamics near the singular locus of
\mu
. Observe that
\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(0)
intersects
D+E
only along the smooth locus
E^{\circ}
of
E
. Explicitly, a neighborhood of
\partial X^{\circ}
near
\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(0)
is modeled on

(7.4)

V\times(S^{3}\setminus p^{-1}(W))\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},p)}V\times\mathbb{(}\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus W),\quad W\text{ a small neighborhood of }\{0,\infty\}.

The
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action is given by
\lambda\cdot(\mathbf{x},z,w)\mapsto(\mathbf{x},\lambda z,\lambda^{-1}w)
, whereas the
S^{1}
-action generated by the Reeb flow is
\eta\cdot(\mathbf{x},z,w)\mapsto(\mathbf{x},\eta z,\eta w)
.

Second,
\lambda^{\circ}
descends under Liouville reduction to a Liouville form
(\underline{X},\underline{\lambda}^{\circ})
, and admits a similar interpretation. This is closely related to the fact that the resolution

\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(0)/\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mu^{-1}(0)/\mathbb{S}^{1}

is a homeomorphism and the left hand side carries a well-defined symplectic form. The inverse image of the singular locus is precisely the divisor
\underline{D}\subset\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(0)/\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The
S^{1}
-action on
p^{-1}(E^{\circ})
generated by the Reeb flow is transverse to the given
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action. Thus, the Reeb flow of
\underline{\lambda}^{\circ}
is periodic of minimal period
2\pi
. This implies
\underline{\lambda}^{\circ}
coincides with the one induced by the presentation of
\underline{X}^{\circ}
as a divisor complement:

\underline{X}^{\circ}\cong\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(0)/\mathbb{S}^{1}-\underline{D}.

Computing Floer cohomologies

We begin with the local model
zw:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
, for which

X^{\circ}=(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2\circ},\underline{X}^{\circ}=\mathbb{C}^{*}.

Our goal is to compare the Floer cohomologies associated to the Lagrangians

L=\mathbb{D}^{2}_{\Re}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad L^{\circ}=L\cap X^{\circ}\subset X^{\circ},\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{L}^{\circ}=L^{\circ}/\mathbb{S}^{1}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}.

Here,
\mathbb{D}_{\Re}
denotes the Lefschetz thimbles over
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. For each
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
, choose Hamiltonians satisfying

(7.5)

\underline{K}_{\nu}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}-(\nu+\epsilon)|u|^{2}+C_{\nu}&(|u|<1)\\
\epsilon|u|^{2}&(|u|>2)\end{array}\right.,\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{l}K_{\nu}(z,w)\coloneqq\underline{K}_{\nu}(zw),\\
K^{\circ}_{\nu}\coloneqq K_{\nu}|_{(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\circ}}.\end{array}

where
u
is the coordinate of the complex plane,
\epsilon>0
is a sufficiently small number, and
C_{\nu}
is a constant depending on
\nu
. The intersection
L\cap\phi^{1}_{K_{\nu}}(L)
consists of two types of components:
\nu
cleanly intersecting circles over
\underline{L}^{\circ}\cap\phi_{\underline{K}^{\circ}_{\nu}}^{1}(\underline{L}^{\circ})
, and the origin. Using the identification in Remark
6.6
, we can explicitly describe the Floer complexes as

(7.6)

\begin{split}CF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L^{\circ},L^{\circ};K_{\nu}^{\circ})&=\mathbb{C}[\lambda,t]\langle 1,x,\ldots x^{\nu}\rangle,\\
CF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L,L;K_{\nu})&=\mathbb{C}[\lambda,t]\langle 1,x,\ldots x^{\nu}\rangle\oplus\mathbb{C}[\lambda]\langle p^{\nu+1}\rangle,\\
\deg x=2,\quad&\deg\lambda=2,\quad\deg t=1,\quad\deg p^{\nu+1}=2\nu+2.\end{split}

Here,
t
denotes a representative of the point class of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
\lambda
is the degree
2
generator of
H_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}^{\bullet}(*)
,
p^{\nu+1}
denotes the fixed point at the origin, and the element
x^{k}
record the number of wrappings around the origin. See Figure
5
. Their degrees are computed using the standard complex volume form
dz
on
\mathbb{C}
. The differentials are given by

\begin{split}\mu^{1}_{L^{\circ}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\nu}c_{k,1}x^{k}t+\sum_{k=0}^{\nu}c_{k,0}x^{k}\right)&=\sum_{k=0}^{\nu}c_{k,1}x^{k}\lambda,\\
\mu^{1}_{L}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\nu}c_{k,1}x^{k}t+\sum_{k=0}^{\nu+1}c_{k,0}x^{k}\right)&=\sum_{k=0}^{\nu}c_{k,1}x^{k}\lambda\pm\sum_{k=0}^{\nu}c_{k,1}x^{k+1},\end{split}

where we set
x^{\nu+1}\coloneqq p^{\nu+1}
for notation convenience (see Figure
5
). The first differential is standard. Indeed, there are no pseudo-holomorphic sections of
zw:X^{\circ}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
with the given Lagrangian boundary condition. Thus, Floer strips are confined to the fibers of this map, which implies
\mu^{1}_{L^{\circ}}(x^{k})=0
. The relation
\mu^{1}_{L^{\circ}}(x^{k}t)=x^{k}\lambda
is an instance of the computation in
[
33
, Lemma 4.4]
.

In contrast, the second differential contains an additional term
\mu^{1}_{L}(x^{k}t)=x^{k}\lambda\pm x^{k+1}
. When
k=\nu
, the strip contributing to
x^{\nu+1}
can be read directly from the base of the fibration: see Figure
5
. Combining these relations, we compute the Floer cohomologies as follows:

(7.7)

\begin{split}HF_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}^{\bullet}(L^{\circ},L^{\circ};K^{\circ}_{\nu})&\simeq\mathbb{C}\langle[1],[x],\ldots,[x^{\nu}]\rangle,\\
HF_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}^{\bullet}(L,L;K_{\nu})&\simeq\mathbb{C}[\lambda],\quad[\lambda^{k}]\sim[x^{k}],\quad k\in 0,\ldots,\nu.\end{split}

The first computation confirms Corollary
6.13
, since it is isomorphic to
HF^{\bullet}(\underline{L}^{\circ},\underline{L}^{\circ};\underline{K}_{\nu})
. The second computation verifies Theorem
4.20
; it is isomorphic to
H_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}^{\bullet}(L)=\mathbb{C}[\lambda]
. Letting
\nu\to\infty
, we conclude that the natural inclusion (which is not a cochain map)

CF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L^{\circ},L^{\circ};K_{\nu}^{\circ})\to CF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L,L;K_{\nu}),\quad x,\lambda,t\mapsto x,\lambda,t,

induces an isomorphism on cohomology, as observed in
[
40
]
. The generators
x
and
\lambda
are intertwined, and we obtain:

(7.8)

\mathbb{C}[x]\simeq\lim_{\nu\to\infty}HF^{\bullet}(\underline{L}^{\circ},\underline{L}^{\circ};\underline{K}_{\nu})\simeq\lim_{\nu\to\infty}HF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L^{\circ},L^{\circ};K_{\nu})\simeq HF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L,L)\simeq\mathbb{C}[\lambda].

Figure 5.
CF^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(L,L;K_{\nu})
for
\nu=0
and
\nu=1
.

This computation generalizes to Lagrangians in a conic fibration.

.

X
